<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> For every sufficiently large prime $`s\equiv2\pmod3`$ and every sufficiently large odd integer <i>n</i>, with the threshold depending on <i>s</i>, we construct an intersecting $`(s^n+1)`$-partite $`(s^n+1)`$-uniform hypergraph with covering number $`s^n+1`$. This disproves Ryser's covering conjecture in its intersecting case.

- **claim-main-ryser-disproof** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-main)): There exists $s_0$ such that, for every prime $s\ge s_0$ with $s\equiv2\pmod3$, there is an integer $n_0(s)\ge3$ with the following property. For every odd integer $n\ge n_0(s)$, put $q=s^n$. There is a finite intersecting $(q+1)$-partite $(q+1)$-uniform hypergraph $H$ satisfying $$\tau(H)=q+1.$$ In particular, the intersecting case of <a href="#eq:ryser" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:ryser">[eq:ryser]</a> is false.
  - Explanation: For large primes congruent to two mod three and large odd exponents, an intersecting uniform partite hypergraph exists whose covering number exceeds the Ryser bound by one, so the intersecting case of the conjecture fails.
- **claim-tree-cover-counterexample** (theorem, [source](#stmt-cor-tree-cover)): Let $s_0$ and $n_0(s)$ be as in Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>. For every prime $s\ge s_0$ with $s\equiv2\pmod3$ and every odd integer $n\ge n_0(s)$, put $r=s^n+1$. There is a finite simple complete graph with an $r$-edge-coloring for which the minimum number of monochromatic trees needed to cover its vertices is exactly $r$. The same minimum holds with connected monochromatic subgraphs or maximal monochromatic components in place of trees.
  - Explanation: Turning the hypergraph into an edge-coloured complete graph shows that covering all vertices by monochromatic trees, connected subgraphs or components needs as many pieces as there are colours, refuting the tree-cover conjecture at these ranks.
- **claim-uniform-incidence-saving** (bound, [source](#stmt-thm-incidence)): There exist absolute constants $\kappa\in(0,1/10)$ and $Q$ with the following property. Suppose that $q\ge Q$ is a prime power and every proper subfield of $\mathbb F_q$ has order at most $q^{1/3}$. If $P$ and $\mathcal L$ are sets of projective points and projective lines over $\mathbb F_q$ satisfying $$\label{eq:inc-theorem-sizes} \|P\|,\|\mathcal L\|\le q^{1+\kappa},$$ then $$\label{eq:inc-theorem-bound} I(P,\mathcal L)\le q^{3/2-\kappa}.$$
  - Explanation: Over finite fields whose proper subfields are all small, point-line incidences between not-too-large sets obey a power saving with constants independent of the field.
- **claim-sumset-estimates** (bound, [source](#stmt-inc-sumset-estimates)): Let the sets below be finite subsets of an abelian group.* 1. *If $Z$ is nonempty, then $$\label{eq:inc-triangle} \|X-Y\|\,\|Z\|\le \|X-Z\|\,\|Z-Y\|.$$* 2. *If $Y$ is nonempty and $\|X-Y\|\le M\|Y\|$, then $X$ is contained in the union of at most $M$ translates of $Y-Y$.* 3. *If $Y$ is nonempty and $\|Y-Y\|\le M\|Y\|$, then, for all nonnegative integers $u,v$, $$\label{eq:inc-multiple-sums} \|uY-vY\|\le M^{u+v}\|Y\|.$$
  - Explanation: Collects the triangle inequality for difference sets, the covering by translates of a difference set, and the multiple-sum bound from small doubling.
- **claim-simultaneous-extraction** (bound, [source](#stmt-inc-simultaneous-extraction)): Let $F$ be a field, let $A,B\subseteq F^*$ be finite, and let $G\subseteq A\times B$. Define $$D=\{a-b:(a,b)\in G\},\qquad J=\{a/b:(a,b)\in G\}.$$ Suppose that, for real numbers $n>0$ and $V\ge2$, $$\label{eq:inc-graph-hypotheses} \|A\|,\|B\|,\|D\|,\|J\|\le Vn, \qquad \|G\|\ge n^2/V.$$ Then there is $S\subseteq A$ such that $$\label{eq:inc-extracted-size} \frac{3n}{8V^2}\le \|S\|\le Vn$$ and $$\label{eq:inc-extracted-expansion} \|S-S\|,\|S/S\|\le 2^{20}V^{22}n.$$ Here $S/S=\{a/a':a,a'\in S\}$.
  - Explanation: From a dense bipartite graph with small difference and quotient label sets one extracts a single subset that has small difference set and small quotient set at once.
- **claim-scalar-coverings** (bound, [source](#stmt-inc-scalar-coverings)): Let $B$ be a nonempty finite subset of a field $F$, let $E\subseteq B$, and let $K\ge2$. Suppose that $$\label{eq:inc-scalar-hypotheses} \|B-B\|\le K\|B\|, \qquad \|B-eB\|\le 2K^3\|B\|\quad(e\in E).$$ Define $$\label{eq:inc-scalar-exponents} C_0=4,\qquad C_{m+1}=3C_m+5, \qquad\text{so that }C_m=\frac{13\cdot3^m-5}{2}.$$ If $\lambda\in F$ has an expression in $E$ using at most $m$ operations, then $\lambda B$ is contained in at most $K^{C_m}$ translates of $B-B$. In particular, $$\label{eq:inc-scalar-sum-bound} \|E+\lambda E\|\le \|B+\lambda B\| \le K^{C_m+3}\|B\|.$$ These bounds are uniform over all such expressions and all choices of their entries from $E$.
  - Explanation: Scalars built from a bounded number of field operations on a good set have dilates covered by few translates of the difference set, uniformly over expressions.
- **claim-no-approximate-subfield** (theorem, [source](#stmt-inc-no-approximate-subfield)): There is no sequence of prime powers $q\to\infty$, each satisfying the proper-subfield hypothesis of Theorem <a href="#thm:incidence" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:incidence">3</a>, together with sets $S\subseteq\mathbb F_q^*$ and numbers $K\ge2$ such that $$\label{eq:inc-approximate-subfield-hypotheses} \|S\|=q^{1/2+o(1)},\qquad K=q^{o(1)},\qquad \|S-S\|,\|S/S\|\le K\|S\|.$$
  - Explanation: Excludes sets of about square-root size with small difference and quotient sets in fields lacking large proper subfields.
- **claim-martingale-concentration** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-martingale)): Let $Z_i$, $1\leq i\leq n$, be martingale differences for a filtration $(\mathcal A_i)_{i=0}^n$. Suppose that $M,V>0$ and deterministic numbers $v_i\geq0$ satisfy, almost surely, $$\|Z_i\|\leq M,\qquad \mathbb E(Z_i^2\mid\mathcal A_{i-1})\leq v_i,\qquad \sum_{i=1}^n v_i\leq V.$$ Then, for every $u>0$, $$\label{eq:lc-martingale} \Pr\left(\left\|\sum_{i=1}^n Z_i\right\|\geq u\right) \leq 2\exp\left[-\frac14 \min\left\{\frac{u^2}{V},\frac{u}{M}\right\}\right].$$
  - Explanation: A Bernstein-type tail bound for sums of bounded martingale differences with controlled conditional variances.
- **claim-line-cover-pencil** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-linecover)): Let $q\to\infty$ through prime powers for which every proper subfield of $\mathbb F_q$ has order at most $q^{1/3}$. Fix a positive integer $D$ and constants $\delta>0$ and $0<\rho<1$. For each $q$, let $\mathcal F$ be a family of at most $Dq^D$ subsets of $\mathbb F_q^2$, called shapes, such that* 1. *every $F\in\mathcal F$ has at most $Dq$ points;* 2. *for every $F\in\mathcal F$ and affine line $\ell$, either $F=\ell$ or $\|F\cap\ell\|\leq D$.* *There are at most $Dq$ independent trials, each producing a random list of at most $D$ shapes from $\mathcal F$. Delete every point on any listed shape, and denote the surviving set by $X$. Assume that* 1. *for each point $x$ and each trial, the probability that this trial deletes $x$ is at most $D/q$;* 2. *$\Pr(x\in X)=\delta_q\geq\delta$ for every $x\in\mathbb F_q^2$;* 3. *for each affine line $\ell$, the sum over trials of the probabilities that the trial lists the exact shape $\ell$ is at most $D/q$.* *Write $X^\ell$ for the surviving set when all exact occurrences of the shape $\ell$ are ignored, with every other listed shape still deleted. With probability tending to one, both of the following statements hold:* 1. *$\|X^\ell\cap\ell\|\geq\delta q/2$ for every affine line $\ell$;* 2. *every set of at most $q$ affine lines covering all but at most $2q$ points of $X$ contains at least $(1-\rho)q$ lines through a common projective point.
  - Explanation: After structured random deletions, every line survives with many points, and any small family of lines covering almost all survivors contains almost a full pencil.
- **claim-compatible-translates** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-selection)): For every positive integer $D$ there is a positive integer $K=K(D)$ with the following property. Let $q$ tend to infinity through prime powers. For each $q$, let $n\le Dq$, and for $1\le i\le n$ let $W_i\subseteq\mathbb F_q^2$ and let $D_i$ be a set of directions, with $$\|W_i\|\le D,\qquad \|D_i\|\le D.$$ Assume that each direction belongs to $D_i$ for at most $D$ indices $i$. For distinct indices $i,j$, declare the translates $b+W_i$ and $b'+W_j$ to conflict if there are $u\in W_i$, $v\in W_j$, and $d\in D_i\cup D_j$ such that $$(b+u)-(b'+v)\in d.$$ For each $i$, draw $K$ independent uniform shifts $b_{i,1},\ldots,b_{i,K}\in\mathbb F_q^2$, independently for all indices. With probability tending to one, one can choose $k_i\in\{1,\ldots,K\}$ for every $i$ so that the translates $b_{i,k_i}+W_i$ have no conflicts. The convergence is uniform over all data satisfying the stated bounds.
  - Explanation: From a bounded number of independent random translates per configuration one can with high probability choose one per configuration with no forbidden joins between different ones.
- **claim-domination-certificate** (theorem, [source](#stmt-sel-domination)): Let a finite simple graph have a vertex partition into nonempty parts $A_1,\ldots,A_n$. If there is no independent set containing one vertex from each part, then there are a nonempty index set $I$ and a set $$T\subseteq\bigcup_{i\in I}A_i, \qquad \|T\|\le 2(\|I\|-1),$$ such that $T$ dominates $\bigcup_{i\in I}A_i$.
  - Explanation: If a partitioned graph has no independent transversal, a small set dominates the union of some subfamily of parts.
- **claim-gadget-configuration** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-gadget)): For every sufficiently large prime $s\equiv2\pmod3$, there is a set $Y\subseteq\mathbb F_s^2$ with the following properties. For each $d\in\mathbb F_s^*$, there is an affine line $\ell_d$ of slope $d$, and $$Y=\mathbb F_s^2\setminus\bigcup_{d\in\mathbb F_s^*}\ell_d.$$ For each $d\in\mathbb F_s^*$, every line of slope $d$ other than $\ell_d$ contains at least five points of $Y$. The map $$f:\mathbb F_s^2\longrightarrow\mathbb F_s^2, \qquad f(x,y)=(x^3,y),$$ is a bijection, and every affine line contains at least $s/8$ points of $f(Y)$. Moreover, if $a$ affine lines and $b$ individual points cover $f(Y)$, and $$\label{con-weighted-budget} a+\frac b2\le s,$$ then $a=s$, $b=0$, and the lines form a full parallel class. Consequently every such mixed cover has weighted cost at least $s$, and no cover of $f(Y)$ by at most $s$ lines contains two nonparallel lines.
  - Explanation: A configuration in a small prime plane exists such that, after the cubing change of coordinates, every line keeps many points and any cheap mixed cover must be a full parallel class.
- **claim-deletion-count** (theorem, [source](#stmt-con-deletion-count)): For every candidate and every $d\in S_i$, exactly $s-1$ lines of $\mathcal B_{i,k}$ have direction $d$. Each of these lines contains at least five points of $Y_{i,k}$. Consequently $\|\mathcal B_{i,k}\|=(s-1)^2$.
  - Explanation: Each candidate copy prescribes an exact number of deletion lines in each altered direction, each with several points of the copy.
- **claim-pool-properties** (theorem, [source](#stmt-con-pool-properties)): There are constants $\delta\in(0,1)$ and $\rho=\delta/10$, independent of $n$, with the following properties with probability tending to one as $n$ tends to infinity through odd integers. Let $$X_{\rm pool} =\mathbb F_q^2\setminus \bigcup_{i\in\mathcal I}\bigcup_{k=1}^K \bigcup_{\ell\in\mathcal B_{i,k}}\ell.$$* 1. *Every cover of $X_{\rm pool}$ by at most $q$ affine lines contains at least $(1-\rho)q$ lines through a common projective point.* 2. *For every affine line $\ell$, if only exact occurrences of $\ell$ are ignored in the pool deletions, at least $\delta q/2$ points of $\ell$ remain.* 3. *For every finite point $p$ and affine line $\ell$ not through $p$, at most five groups $i$ have a candidate $P_{i,k}$ containing a point $y\in\ell$ whose join to $p$ has direction in $S_i$.
  - Explanation: For the whole pool of candidates, covers of surviving points contain almost a pencil, every line retains many survivors, and an external point sees few groups along a line.
- **claim-hypergraph-intersecting** (theorem, [source](#stmt-con-intersection)): The hypergraph $H$ is intersecting. Furthermore, all the edges in its displayed parametrization are distinct.
  - Explanation: All pairs of ordinary and exceptional edges meet, and the displayed parametrization lists distinct edges.
- **claim-label-action** (theorem, [source](#stmt-con-label-action)): Fix a group $i$.* 1. *A special vertex of group $i$ covers the positions on one subfield line. A special vertex of another group covers none.* 2. *An ordinary vertex of direction in $S_i$ covers none. An ordinary vertex of direction in $A_i$, if it covers any positions, covers them on a single horizontal or vertical line in the $f$ coordinates.* 3. *An ordinary vertex of direction outside $S_i\cup A_i$ covers at most one position.* *In particular, the positions covered by any one vertex are contained in a subfield affine line.
  - Explanation: Each vertex covers exceptional positions of a group only along one subfield line, or at most a single position, which reduces covering to lines and points in the small plane.

In particular, $$\\label{eq:inc-scalar-sum-bound}\n |E+\\lambda E|\\le |B+\\lambda B|\n \\le K^{C_m+3}|B|.$$ These bounds are uniform over all such expressions and all choices of their entries from $E$.",
        "source_id": "PM-162.2"
      },

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-inc-no-approximate-subfield",
        "exact_quote": "There is no sequence of prime powers $q\\to\\infty$, each satisfying the proper-subfield hypothesis of Theorem <a href=\"#thm:incidence\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"thm:incidence\">3</a>, together with sets $S\\subseteq\\mathbb F_q^*$ and numbers $K\\ge2$ such that $$\\label{eq:inc-approximate-subfield-hypotheses}\n |S|=q^{1/2+o(1)},\\qquad K=q^{o(1)},\\qquad\n |S-S|,|S/S|\\le K|S|.$$",
        "source_id": "PM-162.2"
      },

          "source_id": "PM-162.2"

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-martingale",
        "exact_quote": "Let $Z_i$, $1\\leq i\\leq n$, be martingale differences for a filtration $(\\mathcal A_i)_{i=0}^n$. Suppose that $M,V>0$ and deterministic numbers $v_i\\geq0$ satisfy, almost surely, $$|Z_i|\\leq M,\\qquad\n \\mathbb E(Z_i^2\\mid\\mathcal A_{i-1})\\leq v_i,\\qquad\n \\sum_{i=1}^n v_i\\leq V.$$ Then, for every $u>0$, $$\\label{eq:lc-martingale}\n \\Pr\\left(\\left|\\sum_{i=1}^n Z_i\\right|\\geq u\\right)\n \\leq 2\\exp\\left[-\\frac14\n   \\min\\left\\{\\frac{u^2}{V},\\frac{u}{M}\\right\\}\\right].$$",
        "source_id": "PM-162.2"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-martingale",
          "exact_quote": "Let $Z_i$, $1\\leq i\\leq n$, be martingale differences for a filtration $(\\mathcal A_i)_{i=0}^n$. Suppose that $M,V>0$ and deterministic numbers $v_i\\geq0$ satisfy, almost surely, $$|Z_i|\\leq M,\\qquad\n \\mathbb E(Z_i^2\\mid\\mathcal A_{i-1})\\leq v_i,\\qquad\n \\sum_{i=1}^n v_i\\leq V.$$ Then, for every $u>0$, $$\\label{eq:lc-martingale}\n \\Pr\\left(\\left|\\sum_{i=1}^n Z_i\\right|\\geq u\\right)\n \\leq 2\\exp\\left[-\\frac14\n   \\min\\left\\{\\frac{u^2}{V},\\frac{u}{M}\\right\\}\\right].$$",
          "source_id": "PM-162.2"
        }
      ],

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-linecover",
        "exact_quote": "Let $q\\to\\infty$ through prime powers for which every proper subfield of $\\mathbb F_q$ has order at most $q^{1/3}$. Fix a positive integer $D$ and constants $\\delta>0$ and $0<\\rho<1$. For each $q$, let $\\mathcal F$ be a family of at most $Dq^D$ subsets of $\\mathbb F_q^2$, called shapes, such that*\n\n1.  *every $F\\in\\mathcal F$ has at most $Dq$ points;*\n\n2.  *for every $F\\in\\mathcal F$ and affine line $\\ell$, either $F=\\ell$ or $|F\\cap\\ell|\\leq D$.*\n\n*There are at most $Dq$ independent trials, each producing a random list of at most $D$ shapes from $\\mathcal F$. Delete every point on any listed shape, and denote the surviving set by $X$. Assume that*\n\n1.  *for each point $x$ and each trial, the probability that this trial deletes $x$ is at most $D/q$;*\n\n2.  *$\\Pr(x\\in X)=\\delta_q\\geq\\delta$ for every $x\\in\\mathbb F_q^2$;*\n\n3.  *for each affine line $\\ell$, the sum over trials of the probabilities that the trial lists the exact shape $\\ell$ is at most $D/q$.*\n\n*Write $X^\\ell$ for the surviving set when all exact occurrences of the shape $\\ell$ are ignored, with every other listed shape still deleted. With probability tending to one, both of the following statements hold:*\n\n1.  *$|X^\\ell\\cap\\ell|\\geq\\delta q/2$ for every affine line $\\ell$;*\n\n2.  *every set of at most $q$ affine lines covering all but at most $2q$ points of $X$ contains at least $(1-\\rho)q$ lines through a common projective point.",
        "source_id": "PM-162.2"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-linecover",
          "exact_quote": "Let $q\\to\\infty$ through prime powers for which every proper subfield of $\\mathbb F_q$ has order at most $q^{1/3}$. Delete every point on any listed shape, and denote the surviving set by $X$. Assume that*\n\n1.  *for each point $x$ and each trial, the probability that this trial deletes $x$ is at most $D/q$;*\n\n2.  *$\\Pr(x\\in X)=\\delta_q\\geq\\delta$ for every $x\\in\\mathbb F_q^2$;*\n\n3.  *for each affine line $\\ell$, the sum over trials of the probabilities that the trial lists the exact shape $\\ell$ is at most $D/q$.*\n\n*Write $X^\\ell$ for the surviving set when all exact occurrences of the shape $\\ell$ are ignored, with every other listed shape still deleted. With probability tending to one, both of the following statements hold:*\n\n1.  *$|X^\\ell\\cap\\ell|\\geq\\delta q/2$ for every affine line $\\ell$;*\n\n2.  *every set of at most $q$ affine lines covering all but at most $2q$ points of $X$ contains at least $(1-\\rho)q$ lines through a common projective point.",
          "source_id": "PM-162.2"
        }

      "exact_statement": "Let $q\\to\\infty$ through prime powers for which every proper subfield of $\\mathbb F_q$ has order at most $q^{1/3}$. With probability tending to one, both of the following statements hold:*\n\n1.  *$|X^\\ell\\cap\\ell|\\geq\\delta q/2$ for every affine line $\\ell$;*\n\n2.  *every set of at most $q$ affine lines covering all but at most $2q$ points of $X$ contains at least $(1-\\rho)q$ lines through a common projective point.",

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-thm-selection",
        "exact_quote": "For every positive integer $D$ there is a positive integer $K=K(D)$ with the following property. Let $q$ tend to infinity through prime powers. For each $q$, let $n\\le Dq$, and for $1\\le i\\le n$ let $W_i\\subseteq\\mathbb F_q^2$ and let $D_i$ be a set of directions, with $$|W_i|\\le D,\\qquad |D_i|\\le D.$$ Assume that each direction belongs to $D_i$ for at most $D$ indices $i$. For distinct indices $i,j$, declare the translates $b+W_i$ and $b'+W_j$ to conflict if there are $u\\in W_i$, $v\\in W_j$, and $d\\in D_i\\cup D_j$ such that $$(b+u)-(b'+v)\\in d.$$ For each $i$, draw $K$ independent uniform shifts $b_{i,1},\\ldots,b_{i,K}\\in\\mathbb F_q^2$, independently for all indices. With probability tending to one, one can choose $k_i\\in\\{1,\\ldots,K\\}$ for every $i$ so that the translates $b_{i,k_i}+W_i$ have no conflicts. The convergence is uniform over all data satisfying the stated bounds.",
        "source_id": "PM-162.2"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-thm-selection",
          "exact_quote": "For every positive integer $D$ there is a positive integer $K=K(D)$ with the following property. Let $q$ tend to infinity through prime powers. For each $q$, let $n\\le Dq$, and for $1\\le i\\le n$ let $W_i\\subseteq\\mathbb F_q^2$ and let $D_i$ be a set of directions, with $$|W_i|\\le D,\\qquad |D_i|\\le D.$$ Assume that each direction belongs to $D_i$ for at most $D$ indices $i$. For distinct indices $i,j$, declare the translates $b+W_i$ and $b'+W_j$ to conflict if there are $u\\in W_i$, $v\\in W_j$, and $d\\in D_i\\cup D_j$ such that $$(b+u)-(b'+v)\\in d.$$ For each $i$, draw $K$ independent uniform shifts $b_{i,1},\\ldots,b_{i,K}\\in\\mathbb F_q^2$, independently for all indices. With probability tending to one, one can choose $k_i\\in\\{1,\\ldots,K\\}$ for every $i$ so that the translates $b_{i,k_i}+W_i$ have no conflicts. The convergence is uniform over all data satisfying the stated bounds.",
          "source_id": "PM-162.2"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "For every positive integer $D$ there is a positive integer $K=K(D)$ with the following property. Let $q$ tend to infinity through prime powers. For each $q$, let $n\\le Dq$, and for $1\\le i\\le n$ let $W_i\\subseteq\\mathbb F_q^2$ and let $D_i$ be a set of directions, with $$|W_i|\\le D,\\qquad |D_i|\\le D.$$ Assume that each direction belongs to $D_i$ for at most $D$ indices $i$. For distinct indices $i,j$, declare the translates $b+W_i$ and $b'+W_j$ to conflict if there are $u\\in W_i$, $v\\in W_j$, and $d\\in D_i\\cup D_j$ such that $$(b+u)-(b'+v)\\in d.$$ For each $i$, draw $K$ independent uniform shifts $b_{i,1},\\ldots,b_{i,K}\\in\\mathbb F_q^2$, independently for all indices. With probability tending to one, one can choose $k_i\\in\\{1,\\ldots,K\\}$ for every $i$ so that the translates $b_{i,k_i}+W_i$ have no conflicts. The convergence is uniform over all data satisfying the stated bounds.",
      "explanation": "From a bounded number of independent random translates per configuration one can with high probability choose one per configuration with no forbidden joins between different ones.",
      "fidelity": "PENDING",
      "hypotheses": [

          "anchor": "stmt-thm-selection",
          "exact_quote": "Let $q$ tend to infinity through prime powers.",
          "source_id": "PM-162.2"

      "id": "claim-compatible-translates",
      "kind": "theorem",
      "quantifiers": [

          "anchor": "stmt-thm-selection",
          "exact_quote": "For every positive integer $D$ there is a positive integer $K=K(D)$",
          "source_id": "PM-162.2"

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-sel-domination",
        "exact_quote": "Let a finite simple graph have a vertex partition into nonempty parts $A_1,\\ldots,A_n$. If there is no independent set containing one vertex from each part, then there are a nonempty index set $I$ and a set $$T\\subseteq\\bigcup_{i\\in I}A_i,\n \\qquad |T|\\le 2(|I|-1),$$ such that $T$ dominates $\\bigcup_{i\\in I}A_i$.",
        "source_id": "PM-162.2"
      },

          "source_id": "PM-162.2"
        }

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-gadget",
        "exact_quote": "For every sufficiently large prime $s\\equiv2\\pmod3$, there is a set $Y\\subseteq\\mathbb F_s^2$ with the following properties. For each $d\\in\\mathbb F_s^*$, there is an affine line $\\ell_d$ of slope $d$, and $$Y=\\mathbb F_s^2\\setminus\\bigcup_{d\\in\\mathbb F_s^*}\\ell_d.$$ For each $d\\in\\mathbb F_s^*$, every line of slope $d$ other than $\\ell_d$ contains at least five points of $Y$. The map $$f:\\mathbb F_s^2\\longrightarrow\\mathbb F_s^2,\n \\qquad f(x,y)=(x^3,y),$$ is a bijection, and every affine line contains at least $s/8$ points of $f(Y)$. Moreover, if $a$ affine lines and $b$ individual points cover $f(Y)$, and $$\\label{con-weighted-budget}\n a+\\frac b2\\le s,$$ then $a=s$, $b=0$, and the lines form a full parallel class. Consequently every such mixed cover has weighted cost at least $s$, and no cover of $f(Y)$ by at most $s$ lines contains two nonparallel lines.",
        "source_id": "PM-162.2"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-gadget",
          "exact_quote": "For every sufficiently large prime $s\\equiv2\\pmod3$, there is a set $Y\\subseteq\\mathbb F_s^2$ with the following properties. The map $$f:\\mathbb F_s^2\\longrightarrow\\mathbb F_s^2,\n \\qquad f(x,y)=(x^3,y),$$ is a bijection, and every affine line contains at least $s/8$ points of $f(Y)$. Moreover, if $a$ affine lines and $b$ individual points cover $f(Y)$, and $$\\label{con-weighted-budget}\n a+\\frac b2\\le s,$$ then $a=s$, $b=0$, and the lines form a full parallel class. Consequently every such mixed cover has weighted cost at least $s$, and no cover of $f(Y)$ by at most $s$ lines contains two nonparallel lines.",
          "source_id": "PM-162.2"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "For every sufficiently large prime $s\\equiv2\\pmod3$, there is a set $Y\\subseteq\\mathbb F_s^2$ with the following properties. For each $d\\in\\mathbb F_s^*$, there is an affine line $\\ell_d$ of slope $d$, and $$Y=\\mathbb F_s^2\\setminus\\bigcup_{d\\in\\mathbb F_s^*}\\ell_d.$$ For each $d\\in\\mathbb F_s^*$, every line of slope $d$ other than $\\ell_d$ contains at least five points of $Y$.       "explanation": "A configuration in a small prime plane exists such that, after the cubing change of coordinates, every line keeps many points and any cheap mixed cover must be a full parallel class.",

      "id": "claim-gadget-configuration",
      "kind": "theorem",
      "quantifiers": [

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **vertex cover** ([source](#eq:ryser)): A vertex cover of a finite hypergraph $H$ is a set of vertices meeting every edge.
- **covering and matching numbers** ([source](#eq:ryser)): Its covering number $\tau(H)$ is the minimum size of such a set, and its matching number $\nu(H)$ is the maximum number of pairwise disjoint edges.
- **r-partite r-uniform hypergraph** ([source](#eq:ryser)): For an integer $r\ge2$, an $r$-partite $r$-uniform hypergraph has disjoint vertex parts $V_1,\ldots,V_r$, with every edge containing exactly one vertex from each part.
- **intersecting hypergraph** ([source](#eq:ryser)): A nonempty hypergraph is intersecting when every two distinct edges meet; equivalently, its matching number is one.
- **monochromatic tree cover** ([source](#pm-h-3)): A monochromatic tree cover is a family of monochromatic trees whose vertex sets cover the graph; the trees may overlap, and singleton trees are allowed.
- **direction** ([source](#pm-h-8)): A direction is a one-dimensional linear subspace of $\mathbb F_q^2$; as a subspace it includes zero.
- **pencil** ([source](#pm-h-8)): A pencil consists of the affine lines through a projective point: it has $q+1$ members for a finite center and $q$ members for a center at infinity.
- **falling factorial** ([source](#pm-h-8)): For a positive integer $j$, write $(a)_j=a(a-1)\cdots(a-j+1)$.
- **expression in a set** ([source](#pm-h-11)): An expression in a set $E\subseteq F$ is built from members of $E\cup\{0,1\}$ using binary addition, subtraction, and multiplication, and unary negation and inversion.

| q-deletion-lines | number of deletion lines per candidate | = | $(s-1)^2$ | every candidate |

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **step-sumset-tools** [claim](#stmt-inc-sumset-estimates) / [proof](#proof-stmt-inc-sumset-estimates): Injective map for the triangle inequality, maximal disjoint translates for covering, minimizing subset argument for multiple sums. -> triangle, covering and multiple-sum estimates
- **step-extraction** [claim](#stmt-inc-simultaneous-extraction) / [proof](#proof-stmt-inc-simultaneous-extraction): Common-neighbourhood walks of length four count edge labels to bound differences and quotients on one subset. -> subset with small difference set and quotient set
- **step-scalar-coverings** [claim](#stmt-inc-scalar-coverings) / [proof](#proof-stmt-inc-scalar-coverings): Induction on expression length tracking the number of translates under each field operation. -> uniform scalar covering and sumset bounds
- **step-no-approximate-subfield** [claim](#stmt-inc-no-approximate-subfield) / [proof](#proof-stmt-inc-no-approximate-subfield): Ratios of differences form a subfield, forced to be the whole field, then an averaging contradiction with the covering bound. -> exclusion of an intermediate approximate subfield
- **step-incidence-theorem** [claim](#stmt-thm-incidence) / [proof](#proof-stmt-inc-no-approximate-subfield-2): Contradiction: prune the incidence graph, find four anchors with many witnesses, read coordinates from four pencils, apply extraction and the exclusion lemma. -> uniform incidence saving
- **step-martingale** [claim](#stmt-lem-martingale) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-martingale): Exponential moment bound for each difference, iteration, Markov inequality with optimized parameter. -> Bernstein-type tail bound
- **step-line-cover** [claim](#stmt-lem-linecover) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-linecover): Concentration of survivors for all admissible line tuples, then low- and high-concurrency sampling inside a fixed cover to reach a contradiction. -> line survival and almost-pencil conclusion
- **step-domination** [claim](#stmt-sel-domination) / [proof](#proof-stmt-sel-domination): Grow a dominating set from a minimal deficient subfamily using minimal transversals. -> small domination certificate
- **step-selection** [claim](#stmt-thm-selection) / [proof](#proof-stmt-sel-domination-2): Union bound over certificates, split into small certificates and large ones handled by chunked exposure and a feasible-density estimate. -> conflict-free choice of translates
- **step-gadget** [claim](#stmt-lem-gadget) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-gadget): Random choice of omitted lines, survival in both coordinate systems via the line-cover lemma, then classification of mixed covers. -> small-plane configuration with rigid covers
- **step-deletion-count** [claim](#stmt-con-deletion-count) / [proof](#proof-stmt-con-deletion-count): Undo translation and linear map to reduce to subfield lines. -> exact deletion line counts
- **step-pool-properties** [claim](#stmt-con-pool-properties) / [proof](#proof-stmt-con-pool-properties): Verify the hypotheses of the line-cover lemma for the whole pool; union bound for the third property. -> pool-level deletion properties
- **step-intersection** [claim](#stmt-con-intersection) / [proof](#proof-stmt-con-intersection): Case analysis over four types of edge pairs using separation and preservation of axis classes by the cubing map. -> intersecting property and distinctness
- **step-label-action** [claim](#stmt-con-label-action) / [proof](#proof-stmt-con-label-action): Read each vertex action off the exceptional edge labels. -> each vertex covers a subfield line or a point
- **step-main-theorem** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#proof-stmt-con-label-action-2): Assume a cover of size at most q, force a pencil, and refute each case of centre: infinite, finite inside a configuration, finite outside all configurations. -> covering number equals q+1
- **step-tree-cover** [claim](#stmt-cor-tree-cover) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cor-tree-cover): Colour each pair of hyperedges by a shared part; covers of the graph give covers of the hypergraph; stars give the upper bound. -> tree cover minimum equals the number of colours

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **inv-covering-number-exceeds-ryser**: For the threshold prime and odd-degree parameters of the main theorem, the constructed intersecting hypergraph has covering number exactly one more than the Ryser bound would allow, so the intersecting case of the covering inequality fails.
- **inv-subfield-hypothesis-incidence**: The uniform incidence saving is available only when every proper subfield of the ambient field is small; this is what odd extension degree supplies.
- **inv-intersecting-hypergraph**: The hypergraph assembled from ordinary and exceptional edges is intersecting.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **anti-use-ryser-bound-on-these-ranks**: Apply the Ryser covering bound for intersecting partite hypergraphs as a guaranteed cover size at ranks of the form field order plus one covered by the main theorem. -> The constructed hypergraph needs strictly more vertices than the bound would give, so a solver relying on that bound returns an undersized cover.
- **anti-use-numerical-threshold**: Read off a concrete smallest prime or smallest odd degree from the theorem and instantiate the construction at that size. -> No explicit threshold is available, so a chosen size may lie below the range where the probabilistic estimates hold.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `not_applicable`. The main result is an existence statement for sufficiently large parameters. It is obtained by random deletions and a probabilistic selection of translates, and the source states that the proof does not give a numerical threshold. There is no finite procedure with fixed inputs whose output could be executed and checked by an independent oracle.

<a id="pm-s5"></a>
## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| software | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper gives an existence proof for a finite hypergraph. It supplies no interface, data format or procedure a program could implement. |
| games_simulation | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Nothing in the paper is a dynamical, stochastic or interactive model to simulate. The random deletions serve only as a proof device. |
| tools | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper produces no checker, solver or generator. Its output is a mathematical disproof, so there is no tool contract to extract. |
| algorithms | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The construction is existential and probabilistic. It relies on selection and concentration arguments, and the thresholds are not given numerically, so no finite procedure with a stated cost can be extracted. |
| data_structures | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No data structure is introduced. The hypergraph vertices are formal labels used only inside the proof. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | There is no computational kernel, arithmetic pipeline or memory-access pattern in the paper to map onto hardware. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No hardness assumption, protocol or security reduction appears. Finite fields are used only for incidence geometry. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | There is no learning problem, loss or data distribution. Probabilistic arguments serve only to prove existence. |
| optimisation_operations_research | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The result is a negative statement about hitting-set style covers. It shows that the covering inequality fails in the intersecting case, so a solver must not assume a covering bound of rank minus one. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The corollary on monochromatic tree covers is a statement about edge-colored complete graphs. It does not model communication, replication or fault tolerance. |
| other | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The result belongs to pure extremal combinatorics and finite geometry. It gives no engineering construction beyond mathematical background. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **limit-asymptotic-threshold** (nonconstructive): The main result holds only for sufficiently large parameters, and the proof gives no numerical threshold. Blocked use: ['Instantiating the construction at a specific small prime or a specific small odd degree', 'Claiming a concrete rank at which the covering inequality is known to fail']
- **limit-prime-congruence** (hypothesis): The base prime must be large and congruent to two modulo three, so that the cubing change of coordinates is a bijection of the small prime plane. Blocked use: ['Applying the construction with a prime not congruent to two modulo three', 'Reusing the cubing coordinates in a field where cubing is not a permutation']
- **limit-proper-subfield-bound** (hypothesis): The uniform incidence saving requires every proper subfield of the ambient field to be small; odd extension degree of a prime supplies this, while large subfields are not covered. Blocked use: ['Using the incidence saving in fields with a large proper subfield, such as even-degree extensions', 'Using the incidence bound for prime-power orders below the absolute threshold']
- **limit-line-cover-subfield** (formal_scope): The almost-pencil conclusion for line covers is proved only along sequences of prime powers whose proper subfields are all small, and only with high probability. Blocked use: ['Concluding that every small cover of survivors contains a pencil in fields with large subfields', 'Treating the conclusion as holding for every single realization of the deletions']
- **limit-selection-probabilistic** (nonconstructive): Compatible translates are shown to exist with probability tending to one, not produced by an explicit procedure. Blocked use: ['Treating the selection as a deterministic algorithm with a guaranteed output', 'Assuming success for a fixed finite number of pools']
- **limit-gadget-existence** (nonconstructive): The small-plane configuration is obtained by random choice of omitted lines for large primes; no explicit configuration is given. Blocked use: ['Writing down an explicit omitted-line choice from the paper', 'Assuming the configuration exists for small primes']
- **limit-tree-cover-ranks** (counterexample_scope): The monochromatic tree cover counterexample is only for colour counts of the form field order plus one with admissible parameters; it says nothing about other numbers of colours. Blocked use: ['Concluding the tree-cover conjecture fails for every number of colours', 'Using the corollary for colour counts outside the stated family']

<a id="pm-formal"></a>
### Formal statement (Lean 4)

Corpus status: formalized in the source corpus (toolchain `leanprover/lean4:v4.34.1`, Mathlib `d13f23b723b8a846827a245b89c10fc7d3f11612`). Not re-checked here; the statements give the exact quantifiers and hypotheses, not a proof.

Scope note of family 162 (`lean/docs/162.md`, verbatim):

> The following describes the scope of the Lean formalization related to the following accompanying paper(s):
>
> - [Balanced counterexamples to Ryser's conjecture at prime orders](../../preprints/Balanced-Counterexamples-to-Rysers-Conjecture-at-Prime-Orders-September-27-2026/paper.pdf)
> - [A counterexample to Ryser's covering conjecture](../../preprints/A-Counterexample-to-Rysers-Covering-Conjecture-September-23-2026/paper.pdf)
>
> ## Scope
>
> Ryser's covering conjecture predicts that an intersecting $r$-partite hypergraph has a vertex cover of size at most $r-1$. The formalization proves that every sufficiently large prime $q$ has a finite intersecting $(q+1)$-partite, $(q+1)$-uniform hypergraph with covering number $q+1$ and exactly $q+1$ nonisolated vertices in each part. Thus the conjecture fails even with equal part sizes. The prime threshold is existential.
>
> Ryser's covering conjecture predicts $\tau\le(r-1)\nu$ for an $r$-partite hypergraph, where $\tau$ and $\nu$ are its covering and matching numbers. The formalized constructions give finite intersecting $r$-partite $r$-uniform hypergraphs with $\nu=1$ and $\tau=r$, contradicting the bound. The ranks have the form $r=p^n+1$: one fixed prime $p\equiv2\pmod3$ works for all sufficiently large prime degrees $n$, and a second result covers every sufficiently large such prime $p$ and every sufficiently large odd $n$, with the degree threshold allowed to depend on $p$. Infinitely many ranks occur. Equal part sizes and numerical thresholds are not asserted.

Comparator source `RyserCovering.lean` (1757 bytes, sha256 `ce109f1b71b5131eae54916ffb0aabd36a3b90eaf0780bf2a58afec1aaf4ebe4`), verbatim:

```lean
import Mathlib

namespace OAI

noncomputable section

namespace RyserCoveringCounterexample

abbrev Hypergraph (V : Type*) := Finset (Finset V)

section

variable {V : Type*} [DecidableEq V]

def IsCover (H : Hypergraph V) (C : Finset V) : Prop :=
  ∀ e ∈ H, ∃ v ∈ C, v ∈ e

def Intersecting (H : Hypergraph V) : Prop :=
  ∀ e ∈ H, ∀ f ∈ H, e ≠ f → (e ∩ f).Nonempty

attribute [-instance] instDecidablePairwiseCoeFinsetOfDecidableEqOfDecidableRel in
def matchings (H : Hypergraph V) : Finset (Hypergraph V) := by
  classical
  exact H.powerset.filter fun M => (M : Set (Finset V)).Pairwise Disjoint

def matchingNumber (H : Hypergraph V) : ℕ := (matchings H).sup Finset.card

def coverCardinalities (H : Hypergraph V) : Set ℕ :=
  {n | ∃ C : Finset V, IsCover H C ∧ C.card = n}

def coverNumber (H : Hypergraph V) : ℕ := sInf (coverCardinalities H)

def PartiteUniform {I : Type*} (part : V → I) (H : Hypergraph V) : Prop :=
  ∀ e ∈ H, ∀ i : I, ∃! v : V, v ∈ e ∧ part v = i

def RPartiteUniform (r : ℕ) (H : Hypergraph V) : Prop :=
  ∃ part : V → Fin r, PartiteUniform part H

end

def CounterexampleRank (r : ℕ) : Prop :=
  ∃ (V : Type) (fin : Fintype V) (dec : DecidableEq V),
    letI : Fintype V := fin
    letI : DecidableEq V := dec
    ∃ H : Hypergraph V,
      0 < r ∧ H.Nonempty ∧ RPartiteUniform r H ∧ Intersecting H ∧
        (∃ C : Finset V, IsCover H C) ∧ matchingNumber H = 1 ∧ coverNumber H = r

theorem exists_prime_eventually_counterexampleRank :
    ∃ p : ℕ, p.Prime ∧ 5 < p ∧ p % 3 = 2 ∧
      ∃ N : ℕ, ∀ n : ℕ, N ≤ n → n.Prime → 2 < n →
        CounterexampleRank (p ^ n + 1) := by
  sorry

end RyserCoveringCounterexample

end

end OAI
```

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


For every sufficiently large prime $`s\equiv2\pmod3`$ and every sufficiently large odd integer <i>n</i>, with the threshold depending on <i>s</i>, we construct an intersecting $`(s^n+1)`$-partite $`(s^n+1)`$-uniform hypergraph with covering number $`s^n+1`$. This disproves Ryser's covering conjecture in its intersecting case.

<a id="pm-h-1"></a>

<a id="pm-h-2"></a>
### Introduction

<a id="eq:ryser"></a>
A vertex cover of a finite hypergraph $H$ is a set of vertices meeting every edge. Its covering number $\tau(H)$ is the minimum size of such a set, and its matching number $\nu(H)$ is the maximum number of pairwise disjoint edges. For an integer $r\ge2$, an $r$-partite $r$-uniform hypergraph has disjoint vertex parts $V_1,\ldots,V_r$, with every edge containing exactly one vertex from each part. Ryser’s covering conjecture asserts that every such hypergraph satisfies $$\label{eq:ryser}
 \tau(H)\le(r-1)\nu(H).$$ A nonempty hypergraph is intersecting when every two distinct edges meet; equivalently, its matching number is one. In this case the conjecture asks for a cover of size at most $r-1$. The vertices of a cover may lie in several different parts, which is the central obstacle in constructing a counterexample.

For $r=2$, inequality <a href="#eq:ryser" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:ryser">[eq:ryser]</a> is Kőnig’s matching theorem. An equivalent formulation appears in Henderson’s 1971 thesis (Henderson 1971) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[Henderson1971](#ref-Henderson1971)]; Best and Wanless discuss the history of its attribution to Ryser (Best and Wanless 2018) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[BestWanless2018](#ref-BestWanless2018)]. Aharoni proved the full tripartite case (Aharoni 2001) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[Aharoni2001](#ref-Aharoni2001)]. In the intersecting case, the results through rank four are due to Gyárfás, and the rank-five result to Tuza, as recorded by Király and Tóthmérész (Király and Tóthmérész 2017) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[KiralyTothmeresz2017](#ref-KiralyTothmeresz2017)]; see also Tuza’s early study of the covering conjecture (Tuza 1983) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[Tuza1983](#ref-Tuza1983)]. Haxell and Scott proved $\tau(H)\le(r-\epsilon)\nu(H)$ for some $\epsilon>0$ when $r=4$ or $5$, without assuming intersection (Haxell and Scott 2012) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[HaxellScott2012](#ref-HaxellScott2012)]. White proved the particular rank-four case $\nu(H)=2$, obtaining $\tau(H)\le6$ (White 2026) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[White2026](#ref-White2026)].[^1] Under the additional assumption that any two distinct edges meet in exactly one vertex, Francetić, Herke, McKay, and Wanless established the intersecting case through rank nine (Francetić et al. 2017) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[FranceticHerkeMcKayWanless2017](#ref-FranceticHerkeMcKayWanless2017)]. Bishnoi, Das, Morris, and Szabó obtained bounds when every pair of edges has a larger prescribed intersection (Bishnoi et al. 2021) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[BDMS2021](#ref-BDMS2021)].

Finite planes supply the classical equality examples. Delete a point and all lines through it from a projective plane of order $q$. Partition the remaining points according to the deleted line through them, and take the remaining lines as hyperedges. The result is intersecting, has $q+1$ parts and covering number $q$. Thus equality in the proposed bound occurs whenever $r-1$ is a prime power. Work of Mansour, Song, and Yuster (Mansour, Song, and Yuster 2009) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[MansourSongYuster2009](#ref-MansourSongYuster2009)] and Aharoni, Barát, and Wanless (Aharoni, Barát, and Wanless 2016) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[AharoniBaratWanless2016](#ref-AharoniBaratWanless2016)] studies the structure and sparsity of equality examples. Abu-Khazneh, Barát, Pokrovskiy, and Szabó exploit the rigidity of minimum covers in a truncated plane and add exceptional edges to obtain further equality examples (Abu-Khazneh et al. 2019) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[AbuKhaznehBaratPokrovskiySzabo2019](#ref-AbuKhaznehBaratPokrovskiySzabo2019)]. In that construction, the number of parts and the covering number both increase by one. Haxell and Scott combine affine planes with intersecting configurations to obtain covering number at least $r-4$ for every sufficiently large $r$, improved to $r-3$ when $r$ is even (Haxell and Scott 2017) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[HaxellScott2017](#ref-HaxellScott2017)].

Our construction also starts from a finite plane and inserts exceptional edges, but keeps one part for each ambient direction. It changes the line labels within these parts to raise the covering number beyond $q$. The following theorem gives the precise field orders for which this can be done.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *There exists $s_0$ such that, for every prime $s\ge s_0$ with $s\equiv2\pmod3$, there is an integer $n_0(s)\ge3$ with the following property. For every odd integer $n\ge n_0(s)$, put $q=s^n$. There is a finite intersecting $(q+1)$-partite $(q+1)$-uniform hypergraph $H$ satisfying $$\tau(H)=q+1.$$ In particular, the intersecting case of <a href="#eq:ryser" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:ryser">[eq:ryser]</a> is false.*

</div>

Every edge of a nonempty intersecting hypergraph is itself a cover, so the assertion $\tau(H)\le q+1$ is automatic. The proof must construct an intersecting hypergraph and exclude every cover of size at most $q$. We first fix any sufficiently large eligible prime $s$, construct a configuration in $\mathbb F_s^2$, and then take $n$ sufficiently large among the odd integers. All subsequent thresholds may depend on that fixed configuration. The result is asymptotic; the proof does not give a numerical threshold.

A companion paper (OpenAI 2026) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[OpenAI2026Balanced](#ref-OpenAI2026Balanced)] proves a different theorem: for every sufficiently large prime $q$, it constructs such a hypergraph with exactly $q+1$ vertices in every part and with every vertex nonisolated. Its construction modifies line partitions directly in the prime plane. Here the field order is a proper odd power, and the proof uses a small-field configuration with a nonlinear change of coordinates. The two constructions give distinct ranges of ranks. The only results imported from the companion are its three elementary estimates in Appendix A: the affine second-moment identity (Lemma A.1), bounded-drift concentration (Lemma A.2), and the exposure bound for independent trials (Lemma A.3).

Lovász proposed the stronger assertion that deleting $r-1$ vertices can always reduce the matching number. Clow, Haxell, and Mohar disproved that assertion at $r=3$ (Clow, Haxell, and Mohar 2026) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[CHM2025](#ref-CHM2025)]; Abiad, Garbe, Povill, and Spiegel subsequently gave an infinite family at $r=3$ and examples at $r=4$ (Abiad et al. 2025) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[AbiadGarbePovillSpiegel2025](#ref-AbiadGarbePovillSpiegel2025)]. These examples are consistent with <a href="#eq:ryser" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:ryser">[eq:ryser]</a>. They exclude a prescribed matching-reduction procedure, whereas Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> violates the covering inequality itself.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### Monochromatic tree covers

An $r$-edge-coloring here assigns exactly one of $r$ colors to each edge. A monochromatic tree cover is a family of monochromatic trees whose vertex sets cover the graph; the trees may overlap, and singleton trees are allowed. Gyárfás’s conjecture asks whether $r-1$ such trees suffice for every $r$-edge-colored complete graph; see (Milićević 2017, Conjecture 1) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[Milicevic2017](#ref-Milicevic2017)]. This is the covering formulation recorded after Theorem 2 in (Erdős, Gyárfás, and Pyber 1991, sec. 3) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[ErdosGyarfasPyber1991](#ref-ErdosGyarfasPyber1991)], not the distinct vertex-disjoint tree-partition conjecture numbered Conjecture 2 there.

<a id="stmt-cor-tree-cover"></a>
<div id="cor:tree-cover" class="corollary">

**Corollary 2**. *Let $s_0$ and $n_0(s)$ be as in Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>. For every prime $s\ge s_0$ with $s\equiv2\pmod3$ and every odd integer $n\ge n_0(s)$, put $r=s^n+1$. There is a finite simple complete graph with an $r$-edge-coloring for which the minimum number of monochromatic trees needed to cover its vertices is exactly $r$. The same minimum holds with connected monochromatic subgraphs or maximal monochromatic components in place of trees.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-tree-cover"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-tree-cover</summary>

*Proof.* Take the hypergraph $H$ from Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>, with parts $V_1,\ldots,V_r$. For $e\in E(H)$, let $x_i(e)$ be the unique vertex in $e\cap V_i$. Form the complete graph $K$ with vertex set $E(H)$. For each pair of distinct hyperedges $e,f$, choose one index $i$ with $x_i(e)=x_i(f)$ and color the edge $ef$ by $i$. Such an index exists because $H$ is intersecting. Only one color is chosen even when the two hyperedges meet in several parts.

Along a color-$i$ path, the value of $x_i$ is constant. Thus every connected color-$i$ subgraph determines a vertex of $V_i$ belonging to every hyperedge indexed by that subgraph. For a singleton, choose any color and the corresponding vertex of its hyperedge. If $t$ connected monochromatic subgraphs cover the vertices of $K$, the at most $t$ hypergraph vertices so chosen meet every edge of $H$. Hence $t\ge\tau(H)=r$.

Conversely, fix a vertex of $K$. The $r$ monochromatic stars centered there, one for each color and allowing singleton stars, cover all vertices of $K$. They give a tree cover of size at most $r$. The lower bound applies to trees and to maximal monochromatic components as well, and the stars can be enlarged to maximal components. This proves all three stated minima. ◻

</details>

Thus Gyárfás’s cover conjecture fails at these extension-field ranks. The examples admit no cover by $r-1$ connected monochromatic subgraphs even without a diameter restriction. They therefore also refute Milićević’s bounded-diameter strengthening (Milićević 2017, Conjecture 6) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[Milicevic2017](#ref-Milicevic2017)], whose diameter bound may depend on $r$, and the $\alpha=1$ case of the conjecture of DeBiasio, Kamel, McCourt, and Sheats (DeBiasio et al. 2021, Conjecture 2.8) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[DeBiasioKamelMcCourtSheats2021](#ref-DeBiasioKamelMcCourtSheats2021)], whose diameter bound depends only on the independence number $\alpha$. Indeed, $\alpha(K)=1$, so the latter conjecture also demands a cover by at most $r-1$ such subgraphs.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### Proof strategy

In the affine-plane model, the parts are indexed by the $q+1$ directions in $\mathbb F_q^2$, and the vertices of a part are the $q$ lines of that direction. A point $x$ defines an edge consisting of its incident line in each direction. The edges of two points intersect at their join, but a full parallel class always gives a cover of size $q$. We retain many of these point edges and add exceptional edges whose labels are changed in some directions. The construction has three tasks: preserve intersection, restrict small covers of the retained point edges, and make the exceptional edges too costly for those covers.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
###### Restricting the ordinary cover.

Delete a bounded number of translated shapes in each of $O(q)$ independent trials. The shapes are lines or curves that meet every line in boundedly many points. Thus a shape has bounded intersection with a line unless it is that line itself. Section <a href="#sec:linecover" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:linecover">3</a> proves that, under explicit uniform survival assumptions, every cover of almost all surviving points by at most $q$ lines contains almost a pencil. Here a pencil is a family of lines through one projective point, including a parallel class when that point is at infinity. Surviving points on each nondeleted line then force every nondeleted member of this pencil into the cover. To obtain the pencil statement over the required extension fields, Section <a href="#sec:incidence" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:incidence">2</a> first proves an incidence saving by the sum–product method of Bourgain, Katz, and Tao (Bourgain, Katz, and Tao 2004) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[BKT2004](#ref-BKT2004)]. Its constants are uniform over fields whose proper subfields all have order at most $q^{1/3}$.

<a id="pm-h-6"></a>
###### One configuration in two coordinates.

In a fixed prime plane $\mathbb F_s^2$, remove one line in each finite nonzero direction and call the remaining set $Y$. The change of coordinates $f(x,y)=(x^3,y)$ is bijective when $s\equiv2\pmod3$. Original line labels determine which ambient point edges must be deleted to intersect the exceptional edges. The labels for altered directions are instead read from the lines through $f(w)$, for $w\in Y$. The map $f$ preserves the horizontal and vertical parallel classes; this ensures intersection within the exceptional configuration. Its nonlinear action on the other lines makes their images curves with at most three intersections with any line. Applying the deletion lemma in these new coordinates yields the crucial obstruction: covering $f(Y)$ by $a$ lines and $b$ individual points with $a+b/2\le s$ requires exactly one full parallel class and no point resources. Section <a href="#sec:gadget" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:gadget">5</a> proves this statement together with the ordinary-coordinate occupancy needed later.

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
###### Placing the configurations and spending the cover budget.

Embed linearly transformed copies of $Y$ in $\mathbb F_q^2$ so that every ambient direction is altered in one or two copies. Arbitrary translates could create disjoint exceptional edges. We therefore sample a fixed number of independent candidate translates for each copy. Section <a href="#sec:selection" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:selection">4</a> proves that one can choose compatible representatives from these pools, using an independent-transversal argument in the tradition of Haxell (Haxell 1995, 2001) <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[Haxell1995](#ref-Haxell1995), [Haxell2001](#ref-Haxell2001)]. Section <a href="#sec:placement" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:placement">6</a> proves the deletion estimates for the entire independent pool before making this selection. Keeping only the selected deletions restores ordinary points, so the needed estimates transfer by deterministic inclusion.

Section <a href="#sec:hypergraph" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:hypergraph">7</a> gives the labels of every edge, proves intersection, and excludes a cover of size at most $q$. After the ordinary edges force a pencil, its center determines the remaining argument. At infinity, the uncovered exceptional configurations require more vertices than the missing parallel lines release. Inside an embedded configuration, the remaining budget would cover $f(Y)$ by at most $s$ lines including two nonparallel axis lines. Outside every configuration, each deleted pencil line supplies five uncovered exceptional edges, while every additional vertex covers at most five. In all three cases the available budget is too small.

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
###### Conventions.

A direction is a one-dimensional linear subspace of $\mathbb F_q^2$; as a subspace it includes zero. The projective completion adds one point at infinity per direction. A pencil consists of the affine lines through a projective point: it has $q+1$ members for a finite center and $q$ members for a center at infinity. Thus concurrence always includes parallel lines. For a positive integer $j$, write $(a)_j=a(a-1)\cdots(a-j+1)$. All asymptotic constants may depend on the parameters explicitly held fixed. An event holds with high probability if its probability tends to one along the specified sequence of fields. The small field is fixed before the ambient field tends to infinity.

<a id="sec:incidence"></a>
### A uniform incidence bound

For a set $P$ of points and a set $\mathcal L$ of lines in $\mathbb P^2(\mathbb F_q)$, write <a id="eq:inc-definition"></a>$$\label{eq:inc-definition}
 I(P,\mathcal L)
 =\bigl|\{(p,\ell)\in P\times\mathcal L:p\in\ell\}\bigr|.$$ Our aim is a power saving with constants independent of the field and its characteristic. In Section <a href="#sec:linecover" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:linecover">3</a>, this estimate will control intersections of a proposed line cover when no projective point lies on many of its lines. This is the incidence input to the conclusion that a small cover must contain almost a full pencil.

<a id="stmt-thm-incidence"></a>
<div id="thm:incidence" class="theorem">

**Theorem 3** (Uniform incidence saving). *There exist absolute constants $\kappa\in(0,1/10)$ and $Q$ with the following property. Suppose that $q\ge Q$ is a prime power and every proper subfield of $\mathbb F_q$ has order at most $q^{1/3}$. If $P$ and $\mathcal L$ are sets of projective points and projective lines over $\mathbb F_q$ satisfying <a id="eq:inc-theorem-sizes"></a>$$\label{eq:inc-theorem-sizes}
 |P|,|\mathcal L|\le q^{1+\kappa},$$ then <a id="eq:inc-theorem-bound"></a>$$\label{eq:inc-theorem-bound}
 I(P,\mathcal L)\le q^{3/2-\kappa}.$$*

</div>

The proof follows the incidence and sum–product strategy of Bourgain, Katz, and Tao (Bourgain, Katz, and Tao 2004, secs. 3–4 and 6) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[BKT2004](#ref-BKT2004)]. Its geometric step turns an incidence configuration with too many incidences into a dense set of pairs whose differences and quotients range over small sets. A graph argument then extracts one subset with both a small difference set and a small quotient set. Finally, scalar coverings show that such a subset cannot have size near the square root of the field order under the proper-subfield hypothesis.

We develop the two algebraic ingredients first, then return to the incidence configuration. The proofs include the simultaneous extraction of additive and multiplicative control and the bounds on scalar expressions that make the argument uniform over fields of nonprime order.

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### Finite-set estimates

The first task is to pass from control of differences and quotients along the edges of a dense bipartite graph to control on all pairs in one subset. We begin with the sumset estimates needed to use the resulting additive control.

For subsets of an abelian group, $X+Y$ and $X-Y$ denote the usual sumset and difference set. For a nonnegative integer $j$, the notation $jY$ in this subsection means a sum of $j$ copies of $Y$, with $0Y=\{0\}$. Later, a field element multiplying a set will always mean field scaling; we will specify explicitly when an integer denotes an iterated sumset.

The first two estimates below are Ruzsa’s triangle inequality and covering lemma; the maximal-translates proof of the latter appears in (Ruzsa 1999, 324) <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[Ruzsa1999](#ref-Ruzsa1999)]. The multiple-sum bound is a Plünnecke–Ruzsa estimate, for which we use Petridis’s minimizing-subset proof (Petridis 2012, Proposition 2.1 and Section 3) <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[Petridis2012](#ref-Petridis2012)].

<a id="stmt-inc-sumset-estimates"></a>
<div id="inc-sumset-estimates" class="lemma">

**Lemma 4** (Triangle, covering, and multiple-sum estimates). *Let the sets below be finite subsets of an abelian group.*

<a id="eq:inc-triangle"></a>
1.  *If $Z$ is nonempty, then $$\label{eq:inc-triangle}
     |X-Y|\,|Z|\le |X-Z|\,|Z-Y|.$$*

2.  *If $Y$ is nonempty and $|X-Y|\le M|Y|$, then $X$ is contained in the union of at most $M$ translates of $Y-Y$.*

<a id="eq:inc-multiple-sums"></a>
3.  *If $Y$ is nonempty and $|Y-Y|\le M|Y|$, then, for all nonnegative integers $u,v$, $$\label{eq:inc-multiple-sums}
     |uY-vY|\le M^{u+v}|Y|.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-inc-sumset-estimates"><summary>Proof of stmt-inc-sumset-estimates</summary>

*Proof.* For the triangle inequality, choose one representation $d=x_d-y_d$ for each $d\in X-Y$. The map $$(d,z)\longmapsto (x_d-z,z-y_d)$$ from $(X-Y)\times Z$ into $(X-Z)\times(Z-Y)$ is injective: summing the two coordinates recovers $d$, after which the chosen representation recovers $z$. This proves <a href="#eq:inc-triangle" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:inc-triangle">[eq:inc-triangle]</a>.

For the covering statement, take a maximal set $T\subseteq X$ for which the translates $t-Y$, $t\in T$, are pairwise disjoint. Their union is contained in $X-Y$, so $|T|\le M$. Maximality implies that for every $x\in X$ some $x-Y$ intersects $t-Y$ with $t\in T$, whence $x\in t+(Y-Y)$.

<a id="eq:inc-petridis-growth"></a>
For the multiple-sum bound, the minimizing-subset argument of Petridis (Petridis 2012) <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[Petridis2012](#ref-Petridis2012)] lets us iterate a one-step growth estimate. Put $Q_0=-Y$ and choose a nonempty $W\subseteq Y$ minimizing $$\mu=\frac{|W+Q_0|}{|W|}.$$ Then $\mu\le M$, and every $W'\subseteq W$, including the empty set, satisfies $|W'+Q_0|\ge\mu|W'|$. We claim that for every finite set $E_0$, $$\label{eq:inc-petridis-growth}
 |W+Q_0+E_0|\le\mu|W+E_0|.$$ The assertion is immediate if $E_0$ is empty. Otherwise order its elements as $e_1,\ldots,e_k$. When the translate $W+e_i$ is added to the preceding translates, let $$W_i'=\{w\in W:w+e_i\in\bigcup_{j<i}(W+e_j)\}.$$ It contributes exactly $|W|-|W_i'|$ new elements. Moreover, $W_i'+Q_0+e_i$ is contained in the union of the preceding enlarged translates $W+Q_0+e_j$. Thus the contribution of $W+Q_0+e_i$ is at most $$|W+Q_0|-|W_i'+Q_0|
 \le\mu\bigl(|W|-|W_i'|\bigr).$$ Summing proves <a href="#eq:inc-petridis-growth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:inc-petridis-growth">[eq:inc-petridis-growth]</a>. Iterating this estimate gives $|W+jQ_0|\le\mu^j|W|$ for every nonnegative integer $j$. We now remove the auxiliary set $W$ by applying <a href="#eq:inc-triangle" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:inc-triangle">[eq:inc-triangle]</a> to $uQ_0,vQ_0$ with intermediate set $-W$: $$|uQ_0-vQ_0|\,|W|
 \le |W+uQ_0|\,|W+vQ_0|
 \le\mu^{u+v}|W|^2.$$ Negation preserves cardinality, so $|uQ_0-vQ_0|=|uY-vY|$. Since $|W|\le|Y|$ and $\mu\le M$, this is <a href="#eq:inc-multiple-sums" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:inc-multiple-sums">[eq:inc-multiple-sums]</a>. ◻

</details>

We next carry out the extraction from a dense graph. The argument uses the common-neighborhood method in Gowers’s quantitative refinement (Gowers 1998, sec. 4, Lemma 11 and Proposition 12) <a id="pm-cite-27"></a>[[Gowers1998](#ref-Gowers1998)] of the Balog–Szemerédi theorem (Balog and Szemerédi 1994) <a id="pm-cite-28"></a>[[BalogSzemeredi1994](#ref-BalogSzemeredi1994)]; see also (Fox and Sudakov 2011, sec. 5, Lemmas 5.1–5.2) <a id="pm-cite-29"></a>[[FoxSudakov2011](#ref-FoxSudakov2011)]. The point is to use the same walks for both additive and multiplicative edge labels. We can therefore obtain both bounds on one subset, which is what the subsequent subfield argument requires. In the geometric application, the edge labels will come from the four pencils through four chosen points.

<a id="stmt-inc-simultaneous-extraction"></a>
<div id="inc-simultaneous-extraction" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:inc-graph-hypotheses"></a>
<a id="eq:inc-extracted-size"></a>
<a id="eq:inc-extracted-expansion"></a>
**Lemma 5** (Simultaneous difference and quotient extraction). *Let $F$ be a field, let $A,B\subseteq F^*$ be finite, and let $G\subseteq A\times B$. Define $$D=\{a-b:(a,b)\in G\},\qquad
 J=\{a/b:(a,b)\in G\}.$$ Suppose that, for real numbers $n>0$ and $V\ge2$, $$\label{eq:inc-graph-hypotheses}
 |A|,|B|,|D|,|J|\le Vn,
 \qquad |G|\ge n^2/V.$$ Then there is $S\subseteq A$ such that $$\label{eq:inc-extracted-size}
 \frac{3n}{8V^2}\le |S|\le Vn$$ and $$\label{eq:inc-extracted-expansion}
 |S-S|,|S/S|\le 2^{20}V^{22}n.$$ Here $S/S=\{a/a':a,a'\in S\}$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-inc-simultaneous-extraction"><summary>Proof of stmt-inc-simultaneous-extraction</summary>

*Proof.* We first find a large set of vertices such that every ordered pair is joined by many walks of length four. Counting the possible edge labels of these walks will then bound both differences and quotients of the endpoints.

Regard $G$ as a bipartite graph, and put $\tau=(512V^8)^{-1}$. Call an ordered pair $(a,a_2)\in A^2$ bad if it has fewer than $\tau n$ common neighbors in $B$. To find a neighborhood with few bad pairs, first note that vertices of $B$ with fewer than $n/(2V^2)$ neighbors account for at most $|B|n/(2V^2)\le n^2/(2V)$ edges. Since every vertex of $B$ has at most $Vn$ neighbors, the set of vertices with at least $n/(2V^2)$ neighbors has size at least $n/(2V^2)$.

Summed over all $b\in B$, the number of bad ordered pairs in the neighborhood of $b$ is at most $$|A|^2\tau n\le V^2\tau n^3.$$ Averaging over the large neighborhoods just identified, one obtains a neighborhood $A_0$ of size at least $n/(2V^2)$ in which the number of bad ordered pairs is at most $$2V^4\tau n^2=\frac{n^2}{256V^4}
 \le \frac{|A_0|^2}{64}
 \le \frac{|A_0|^2}{16}.$$ Let $S$ consist of those $a\in A_0$ having at most $|A_0|/4$ bad partners in $A_0$. At most $|A_0|/4$ elements are discarded, so $|S|\ge3|A_0|/4$, proving <a href="#eq:inc-extracted-size" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:inc-extracted-size">[eq:inc-extracted-size]</a>.

<a id="eq:inc-walk-count"></a>
The choice of $S$ ensures that, for every $a,a'\in S$, at least $|A_0|/2$ elements $a_2\in A_0$ form nonbad pairs with both endpoints. Each such $a_2$ gives at least $(\tau n)^2$ ordered walks $$a,b_1,a_2,b_2,a'$$ whose four edges belong to $G$. Repeated vertices are allowed. Consequently each ordered endpoint pair in $S^2$ has at least $$\label{eq:inc-walk-count}
 \frac{|A_0|}{2}(\tau n)^2
 \ge\frac{n^3}{2^{20}V^{18}}$$ such walks. This lower bound now converts the limited number of edge labels into a bound on endpoint differences.

For one of these walks, write its edge differences, always taking the $A$ coordinate minus the $B$ coordinate, as $$d_1=a-b_1,\quad d_2=a_2-b_1,\quad
 d_3=a_2-b_2,\quad d_4=a'-b_2.$$ They satisfy $a-a'=d_1-d_2+d_3-d_4$. For a fixed endpoint pair, the four differences determine the walk uniquely: starting from $a$, one recovers successively $b_1,a_2,b_2,a'$. Choose one ordered endpoint pair for each element of $S-S$. The resulting collections of difference quadruples are disjoint for different differences, because their alternating sums differ. Counting in $D^4$ and using <a href="#eq:inc-walk-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:inc-walk-count">[eq:inc-walk-count]</a> gives $$|S-S|\le
 \frac{|D|^4}{n^3/(2^{20}V^{18})}
 \le 2^{20}V^{22}n.$$

The same walks give the quotient bound on the same set $S$. Put $$j_1=a/b_1,\quad j_2=a_2/b_1,\quad
 j_3=a_2/b_2,\quad j_4=a'/b_2.$$ All are nonzero, and $a/a'=j_1j_3/(j_2j_4)$. From a fixed initial $a$ and these four quotients one recovers $b_1=a/j_1$, $a_2=j_2b_1$, $b_2=a_2/j_3$, and $a'=j_4b_2$. Choosing one endpoint pair for each element of $S/S$ and counting in $J^4$ therefore proves the other bound in <a href="#eq:inc-extracted-expansion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:inc-extracted-expansion">[eq:inc-extracted-expansion]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
#### Bounded scalar expressions and subfields

We now turn to the algebraic obstruction: a set near the square-root field scale cannot have both the small difference set and the small quotient set supplied by the extraction lemma. The first step is to bound sums involving scalars formed using a bounded number of field operations. We use the scalar-covering method of Bourgain, Katz, and Tao (Bourgain, Katz, and Tao 2004, Lemma 3.2 and Proposition 3.3) <a id="pm-cite-30"></a>[[BKT2004](#ref-BKT2004)], keeping the dependence on the number of operations explicit.

An expression in a set $E\subseteq F$ is built from members of $E\cup\{0,1\}$ using binary addition, subtraction, and multiplication, and unary negation and inversion. Each of these operations counts as one operation. Inversion is allowed only when its argument is nonzero.

<a id="stmt-inc-scalar-coverings"></a>
<div id="inc-scalar-coverings" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:inc-scalar-hypotheses"></a>
<a id="eq:inc-scalar-exponents"></a>
**Lemma 6** (Uniform scalar coverings). *Let $B$ be a nonempty finite subset of a field $F$, let $E\subseteq B$, and let $K\ge2$. Suppose that $$\label{eq:inc-scalar-hypotheses}
 |B-B|\le K|B|,
 \qquad |B-eB|\le 2K^3|B|\quad(e\in E).$$ Define $$\label{eq:inc-scalar-exponents}
 C_0=4,\qquad C_{m+1}=3C_m+5,
 \qquad\text{so that }C_m=\frac{13\cdot3^m-5}{2}.$$ If $\lambda\in F$ has an expression in $E$ using at most $m$ operations, then $\lambda B$ is contained in at most $K^{C_m}$ translates of $B-B$. In particular, <a id="eq:inc-scalar-sum-bound"></a>$$\label{eq:inc-scalar-sum-bound}
 |E+\lambda E|\le |B+\lambda B|
 \le K^{C_m+3}|B|.$$ These bounds are uniform over all such expressions and all choices of their entries from $E$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-inc-scalar-coverings"><summary>Proof of stmt-inc-scalar-coverings</summary>

*Proof.* Put $H=B-B$, so $H=-H$ and $0\in H$. In the remainder of this proof, integer multiples of $B$ or $H$ inside sumsets denote iterated sums. We prove the covering assertion by induction on expression length. To combine coverings at an induction step, we will need a fixed covering of $2H$ by translates of $H$. By Lemma <a href="#inc-sumset-estimates" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="inc-sumset-estimates">4</a>, $$|2H-B|=|2B-3B|\le K^5|B|.$$ The covering statement of that lemma consequently supplies a set $T_*\subseteq F$ with $|T_*|\le K^5$ such that <a id="eq:inc-two-differences-cover"></a>$$\label{eq:inc-two-differences-cover}
 2H\subseteq T_*+H.$$

Every $e\in E$ has a covering $eB\subseteq T_e+H$ with $|T_e|\le2K^3\le K^4$, by <a href="#eq:inc-scalar-hypotheses" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:inc-scalar-hypotheses">[eq:inc-scalar-hypotheses]</a> and the covering lemma. The scalars $0$ and $1$ also have coverings by one translate of $H$: use $\{0\}\subseteq H$ and $B\subseteq b_0+H$ for any $b_0\in B$. This proves the case $m=0$.

We now check how the number of translates changes under each allowed operation. Suppose that $$\lambda B\subseteq T+H,\qquad
 \mu B\subseteq U+H,\qquad
 |T|\le K^a,\quad |U|\le K^b.$$ Negation replaces $T$ by $-T$. For addition and subtraction, $$(\lambda\pm\mu)B
 \subseteq \lambda B\pm\mu B
 \subseteq T\pm U+2H
 \subseteq T\pm U+T_*+H,$$ so the number of translates is at most $K^{a+b+5}$. For a product, $$\lambda\mu B
 \subseteq \lambda U+\lambda H
 =\lambda U+(\lambda B-\lambda B)
 \subseteq\lambda U+T-T+T_*+H.$$ This uses at most $K^{b+2a+5}$ translates and is valid even if $\lambda=0$. Finally, if $\lambda\ne0$, then $$|\lambda B-B|
 \le |T|\,|H-B|
 =|T|\,|B-2B|
 \le K^{a+3}|B|.$$ Scaling by $\lambda^{-1}$ and then negating gives $|\lambda^{-1}B-B|\le K^{a+3}|B|$. The covering lemma now covers $\lambda^{-1}B$ by at most $K^{a+3}$ translates of $H$.

These bounds give the claimed uniform induction: in an expression with at most $m+1$ operations, each immediate subexpression has at most $m$ operations, so every resulting covering uses at most $K^{3C_m+5}$ translates. This proves the covering assertion with <a href="#eq:inc-scalar-exponents" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:inc-scalar-exponents">[eq:inc-scalar-exponents]</a>.

To deduce the sumset bound from the covering, let $\lambda B\subseteq T+H$ be the covering just obtained. Then $$|B+\lambda B|
 \le |T|\,|B+H|
 =|T|\,|2B-B|
 \le K^{C_m+3}|B|,$$ again by Lemma <a href="#inc-sumset-estimates" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="inc-sumset-estimates">4</a>. Since $E\subseteq B$, this also bounds $|E+\lambda E|$. ◻

</details>

When working along a sequence $q\to\infty$, a positive quantity $K=q^{o(1)}$ means that $\log K/\log q\to0$. Likewise, $|S|=q^{1/2+o(1)}$ means that $\log|S|/\log q\to1/2$. In particular, every fixed power of a subpower factor is still a subpower factor. This is why a uniform bound for expressions of fixed length suffices for the next lemma.

<a id="stmt-inc-no-approximate-subfield"></a>
<div id="inc-no-approximate-subfield" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:inc-approximate-subfield-hypotheses"></a>
**Lemma 7** (Exclusion of an intermediate approximate subfield). *There is no sequence of prime powers $q\to\infty$, each satisfying the proper-subfield hypothesis of Theorem <a href="#thm:incidence" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:incidence">3</a>, together with sets $S\subseteq\mathbb F_q^*$ and numbers $K\ge2$ such that $$\label{eq:inc-approximate-subfield-hypotheses}
 |S|=q^{1/2+o(1)},\qquad K=q^{o(1)},\qquad
 |S-S|,|S/S|\le K|S|.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-inc-no-approximate-subfield"><summary>Proof of stmt-inc-no-approximate-subfield</summary>

*Proof.* Suppose that such a sequence exists. We first use multiplicative overlaps to obtain sets satisfying the scalar-covering hypotheses. Ratios of differences in a large subset will then form a subfield. The proper-subfield hypothesis will force that subfield to be the whole field, where averaging will contradict the scalar-covering bound.

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
###### Extracting scalar-covering hypotheses.

Put $s=|S|$. The $s$ sets $S/a$, $a\in S$, each have size $s$, and their union is contained in $S/S$. Cauchy–Schwarz gives <a id="eq:inc-multiplicative-overlaps"></a>$$\label{eq:inc-multiplicative-overlaps}
 \sum_{a,b\in S}|S/a\cap S/b|
 \ge\frac{s^4}{|S/S|}\ge\frac{s^3}{K}.$$ Thus some $b\in S$ satisfies $\sum_{a\in S}|S/a\cap S/b|\ge s^2/K$. Since every summand is at most $s$, at least $s/(2K)$ values $a\in S$ have $|S/a\cap S/b|\ge s/(2K)$: otherwise the sum of the large summands would be less than $s^2/(2K)$ and that of all the other summands at most $s^2/(2K)$.

<a id="eq:inc-overlap-subset"></a>
Let $E$ be the set of $e=a/b$ for these values of $a$, and put $B=S/b$. Then $$\label{eq:inc-overlap-subset}
 E\subseteq B,\qquad |B|=s,\qquad
 |E|\ge\frac{s}{2K},\qquad
 |S\cap eS|\ge\frac{s}{2K}\quad(e\in E).$$ The last inequality follows by multiplying $S/a\cap S/b$ by $a$. For $Z=S\cap eS$, the triangle inequality and the containments $Z\subseteq S,eS$ imply $$|S-eS|
 \le\frac{|S-Z|\,|Z-eS|}{|Z|}
 \le\frac{(Ks)^2}{s/(2K)}=2K^3s.$$ After scaling by $1/b$, we have $$|B-B|\le Ks,\qquad |B-eB|\le2K^3s\quad(e\in E).$$ Thus Lemma <a href="#inc-scalar-coverings" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="inc-scalar-coverings">6</a> applies to $B$ and $E$. We have obtained uniform control for every scalar expressible using a bounded number of operations on elements of $E$.

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
###### Ratios of differences form a subfield.

<a id="eq:inc-ratio-set"></a>
Write $t=|E|$. By <a href="#eq:inc-overlap-subset" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:inc-overlap-subset">[eq:inc-overlap-subset]</a> and <a href="#eq:inc-approximate-subfield-hypotheses" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:inc-approximate-subfield-hypotheses">[eq:inc-approximate-subfield-hypotheses]</a>, $t=q^{1/2+o(1)}$. In particular, $t\ge2$ for all sufficiently large fields in the sequence. Define $$\label{eq:inc-ratio-set}
 R_0=\left\{\frac{x-x'}{y-y'}:
 x,x',y,y'\in E,\ y\ne y'\right\}.$$ To prove closure under addition and multiplication, we apply the scalar-covering bound to the result of either operation. Every member of $R_0$ has an expression in $E$ with at most four operations. Consequently, for $r,r'\in R_0$, each of $r+r'$ and $rr'$ has an expression with at most nine operations. For either candidate $\lambda$, Lemma <a href="#inc-scalar-coverings" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="inc-scalar-coverings">6</a> gives $$|E+\lambda E|\le K^{C_9+3}s<t^2$$ for all sufficiently large $q$, uniformly over $r,r'$. Indeed, the ratio of this upper bound to $t^2$ is at most $4K^{C_9+5}/s$, which tends to zero. Therefore the map $(x,y)\mapsto x+\lambda y$ from $E^2$ is not injective. A collision between distinct pairs satisfies $y\ne y'$, since otherwise also $x=x'$, and gives $$\lambda=\frac{x'-x}{y-y'}\in R_0.$$ It follows that $R_0$ is closed under addition and multiplication. It contains $0$ and $1$ and, directly from its definition, is closed under negation and reciprocals of nonzero elements. Thus $R_0$ is a subfield of $\mathbb F_q$.

The size of $E$ now prevents this subfield from being proper. Choose distinct $e_0,e_1\in E$. The map $$e\longmapsto\frac{e-e_0}{e_1-e_0}$$ injects $E$ into $R_0$, so $|R_0|\ge t>q^{1/3}$ for large $q$. The proper-subfield hypothesis forces $R_0=\mathbb F_q$.

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
###### A scalar with a large sumset.

Every field element consequently has a four-operation representation using elements of $E$. The scalar-covering estimate therefore bounds $|E+\lambda E|$ for every scalar. To reach a contradiction, we average over the field and find a scalar for which this sumset is too large.

<a id="eq:inc-large-dilate-sum"></a>
For $\lambda\in\mathbb F_q$, let $N_\lambda$ be the number of ordered quadruples $(x,y,x',y')\in E^4$ satisfying $x+\lambda y=x'+\lambda y'$. There are $t^2$ identical pairs, which give a collision for every $\lambda$. If $y\ne y'$, exactly one value of $\lambda$ gives a collision; if $y=y'$ but $x\ne x'$, none does. Hence <a id="eq:inc-average-collisions"></a>$$\label{eq:inc-average-collisions}
 \frac1q\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbb F_q}N_\lambda
 =t^2+\frac{t^4-t^3}{q}
