The order of choices is $s,Y$, then the selection constant and $K$, then the deletion constants $D,\delta,\rho$, and finally $q=s^n$. Only after fixing these constants do we require $q=s^n$ to be sufficiently large. All further numerical inequalities below involve these fixed constants and can be imposed by enlarging this final threshold. Thus the construction is available for every sufficiently large odd integer $n$, with the same $s,Y$; its pools and selected shifts may depend on $n$. The constant used for the ambient deletion lemma may depend on $K$, but it changes no hypothesis of the already applied selection theorem. A small value of $\delta$ only increases the final lower threshold on $q$.

<a id="sec:hypergraph"></a>
### The hypergraph and its covering number

The placement supplies the ordinary points $X$ and the exceptional copies $Y_i$. We now turn them into edges. Ordinary edges retain all their affine-line labels. In an exceptional copy, the directions $S_i$ instead use line labels read from $f(Y)$; all remaining directions retain their ordinary labels. We will first check that every pair of edges still meets, then use the rigidity of $f(Y)$ to rule out covers of size $q$.

<a id="pm-h-43"></a>
#### Vertices, edges, and intersection

Set $r=q+1$. There is one vertex part $V_d$ for each $d\in\mathbb P^1(\mathbb F_q)$. For every ambient affine line $\ell$ of direction $d$, place an ordinary vertex $o_\ell$ in $V_d$. If $d\in S_i$, let $t_i(d)\in\mathbb F_s^*$ be the unique subfield direction whose image under $M_i$ is $d$. For each of the $s$ subfield affine lines $L$ of direction $t_i(d)$, place a special vertex $v_{i,d,L}$ in $V_d$. These subfield lines are read in the new coordinates used for $f(Y)$. All vertices are formal labels, tagged to be distinct between parts, between ordinary and special types, and between different groups. In particular each $V_d$ is finite, and the parts are pairwise disjoint.

<a id="con-exceptional-vertex"></a>
Write $\ell_d(z)$ for the ambient line of direction $d$ through $z$, and $L_t(w)$ for the subfield line of direction $t$ through $w\in\mathbb F_s^2$. Each $x\in X$ defines the ordinary edge $$e_x=\{o_{\ell_d(x)}:d\in\mathbb P^1(\mathbb F_q)\}.$$ For each $i\in\mathcal I$ and $w\in Y$, put $y_{i,w}=b_i+M_iw$ and define an exceptional edge by selecting, in part $V_d$, the vertex $$\label{con-exceptional-vertex}
 \begin{cases}
 v_{i,d,L_{t_i(d)}(f(w))},& d\in S_i,\\
 o_{\ell_d(y_{i,w})},&d\notin S_i.
 \end{cases}$$ Denote this edge by $e_{i,w}$, and let $$H=\{e_x:x\in X\}\cup
   \{e_{i,w}:i\in\mathcal I,\ w\in Y\}.$$ Thus an exceptional edge changes labels in the assigned directions, without introducing any new part. Every edge contains exactly one vertex from every part, so $H$ is $(q+1)$-partite and $(q+1)$-uniform. It is nonempty, because $Y\ne\varnothing$.

Figure <a href="#fig:two-coordinates" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:two-coordinates">1</a> shows how to read an exceptional edge from its parameter $w$. The deletion lines were defined from its ordinary base point $y_{i,w}$, while its special labels use $f(w)$. The two axis parallel classes are preserved by $f$, which joins these descriptions in the intersection proof.

<figure id="fig:two-coordinates">

<figcaption>The two label rules for the vertex of $e_{i, w}$ in part $V_{d}$. The left branch uses the embedded point $b_{i} + M_{i}w$; the right branch uses $f(w) = (w_{1}^{3},w_{2})$ in the small plane. For each $d ∈ A_{i}$, two parameters give the same ordinary label exactly when their images under $f$ lie on the same corresponding horizontal or vertical line. The diagram records maps and label choices, rather than geometric positions in the finite plane.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-two-coordinates"></a>
**Figure source and visual witness** (`fig:two-coordinates`)

  >=Latex,
  point/.style={draw,rounded corners=2pt,align=center,inner sep=7pt},
  labelbox/.style={draw,align=center,text width=5.1cm,inner sep=7pt},
  every node/.style={font=\small}]
\node[point] (w) at (0,0) {$w=(w_1,w_2)\in Y$};
\node[point] (old) at (-3.5,-1.55)
  {Ambient coordinates\\[3pt]$y_{i,w}=b_i+M_iw\in Y_i$};
\node[point] (new) at (3.5,-1.55)
  {Small-plane coordinates\\[3pt]$f(w)=(w_1^3,w_2)\in f(Y)$};
\node[labelbox] (ordinary) at (-3.5,-3.65)
  {Ordinary vertex in $V_d$\\[3pt]$o_{\ell_d(y_{i,w})}$};
\node[labelbox] (special) at (3.5,-3.65)
  {Special vertex in $V_d$\\[3pt]$v_{i,d,L_{t_i(d)}(f(w))}$};
\draw[->] (w) -- node[above left,inner sep=2pt,xshift=-4pt,yshift=-2pt] {embed} (old);
\draw[->] (w) -- node[above right,inner sep=2pt,xshift=4pt,yshift=-2pt] {apply $f$} (new);
\draw[->] (old) -- node[left] {$d\notin S_i$} (ordinary);

An arrow labeled "embed" branches down-left to a box containing "Ambient coordinates\n$y_{i,w} = b_i + M_i w \in Y_i$". From that box, a downward arrow labeled "$d \notin S_i$" leads to a box containing "Ordinary vertex in $V_d$\n$o_{\ell_d(y_{i,w})}$". <a id="stmt-con-intersection"></a>
<div id="con-intersection" class="proposition">

**Proposition 15**. *The hypergraph $H$ is intersecting. Furthermore, all the edges in its displayed parametrization are distinct.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-con-intersection"><summary>Proof of stmt-con-intersection</summary>

*Proof.* There are four types of pairs, according to whether their edges are ordinary or exceptional and, in the latter case, whether their group indices agree.

Two ordinary edges with distinct base points share the ordinary label of their join. Next, an exceptional base point $y\in Y_i$ lies on a deletion line in every direction of the nonempty set $S_i$, so $y\notin X$. Its join with $x\in X$ cannot have direction in $S_i$: otherwise that join would be a selected deletion line containing $x$. Its ordinary label therefore belongs to both the exceptional edge and $e_x$.

For exceptional edges from distinct groups $i,j$, the base points are distinct by <a href="#con-separation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="con-separation">[con-separation]</a>. That same separation condition ensures that their join has direction outside both $D_i$ and $D_j$, and hence outside $S_i\cup S_j$. The two edges share its ordinary label.

Finally, consider $e_{i,w}$ and $e_{i,w'}$ with $w\ne w'$. The transformed points $f(w),f(w')$ are distinct. If their join has finite nonzero slope $t$, the exceptional edges share the special label of this subfield line in the part of direction $M_i(t)$. If their join is horizontal or vertical, the relevant original coordinates are equal as well, because $f$ preserves these two parallel classes bijectively. The original base points then share an ordinary axis-direction label, in a direction of $A_i$. This completes the intersection proof.

We also check that the parameters count distinct edges, as required when we count exceptional edges below. Two ordinary labels in nonparallel directions determine the ordinary base point, so ordinary edges are distinct. An exceptional edge has special labels and cannot equal an ordinary edge. Within one group, the special labels in any two distinct directions of $S_i$ determine $f(w)$, since the corresponding subfield lines are nonparallel. As $|S_i|=s-1\ge2$, equal exceptional edges within a group would imply $w=w'$. Finally, any special label tagged by group $i$ is absent from every edge of a different group. Exceptional edges in distinct groups are therefore distinct too. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-44"></a>
#### Covering vertices in the small-plane coordinates

We identify the positions of the exceptional edges of group $i$ with $f(Y)$, via $e_{i,w}\leftrightarrow f(w)$. The action of a covering vertex means the subset of these positions whose edges contain that vertex. The next lemma translates every such action into one of the two resources in Lemma <a href="#lem:gadget" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:gadget">12</a>: a subfield line or an individual point.

<a id="stmt-con-label-action"></a>
<div id="con-label-action" class="lemma">

**Lemma 16**. *Fix a group $i$.*

1.  *A special vertex of group $i$ covers the positions on one subfield line. A special vertex of another group covers none.*

2.  *An ordinary vertex of direction in $S_i$ covers none. An ordinary vertex of direction in $A_i$, if it covers any positions, covers them on a single horizontal or vertical line in the $f$ coordinates.*

3.  *An ordinary vertex of direction outside $S_i\cup A_i$ covers at most one position.*

*In particular, the positions covered by any one vertex are contained in a subfield affine line.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-con-label-action"><summary>Proof of stmt-con-label-action</summary>

*Proof.* The assertions for special vertices and altered ordinary directions follow directly from <a href="#con-exceptional-vertex" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="con-exceptional-vertex">[con-exceptional-vertex]</a>. For an axis direction, undoing $b_i,M_i$ fixes one original coordinate, so applying $f$ gives the corresponding horizontal or vertical line. A line in a direction outside $S_i\cup A_i$ cannot contain two points of $P_i$, since the join directions of that embedded subplane are exactly $S_i\cup A_i$. It therefore covers at most one exceptional position. A singleton can be included in an arbitrary subfield line through it, and an empty set requires no covering resource. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-45"></a>
#### The final cover argument

<details id="proof-stmt-con-label-action-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-con-label-action</summary>

*Completion of the proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.* We prove that $H$ has no vertex cover of size at most $q$. Suppose to the contrary that $C$ is such a cover. Its ordinary vertices, regarded as affine lines, must cover $X$: special vertices belong to no ordinary edge. These lines also cover $X_{\rm pool}$. Lemma <a href="#con-pool-properties" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="con-pool-properties">14</a> therefore gives a projective point $p$ such that at least $(1-\rho)q$ ordinary vertices of $C$ are lines through $p$. The line-survival bound will force every undeleted member of this pencil into $C$. We then count the vertices left for exceptional edges, separating centers at infinity, centers inside one of the sets $P_i$, and finite centers outside them all.

<a id="pm-h-46"></a>
###### Every undeleted line in this pencil is forced.

<a id="con-forced-pencil"></a>
We claim that $$\label{con-forced-pencil}
 p\in\ell,\quad\ell\notin B
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad o_\ell\in C,$$ where incidence with an infinite $p$ means that $\ell$ has the corresponding direction. If $o_\ell$ were missing, all other lines of $C$ through $p$ would cover at most one affine point of $\ell$. There are at most $\rho q$ vertices outside the large pencil subcollection. Each of their ordinary lines covers at most one point of $\ell$, and their special vertices cover no ordinary edge. Consequently $$|X\cap\ell|\le1+\rho q.$$ This contradicts <a href="#con-selected-line-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="con-selected-line-bound">[con-selected-line-bound]</a>, since $\delta q/2>1+\rho q$ for sufficiently large $q$ and $\rho=\delta/10$. At infinity the pencil lines are parallel, so the possible contribution of one central point is absent; the same upper bound still applies.

Let $F_p$ be the set of ordinary vertices forced by <a href="#con-forced-pencil" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="con-forced-pencil">[con-forced-pencil]</a>, and call the vertices of $C\setminus F_p$ the additional vertices. The deleted pencil lines determine how many additional vertices the assumed budget $|C|\le q$ permits.

<a id="pm-h-47"></a>
###### An infinite center.

Suppose $p$ is the point at infinity in direction $d$. Put $$I_d=\{i\in\mathcal I:d\in S_i\},
 \qquad z=|I_d|\in\{1,2\}.$$ Only these groups contribute deletion lines in direction $d$, and each contributes $s-1$, by Lemma <a href="#con-deletion-count" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="con-deletion-count">13</a>. At most $z(s-1)$ of the $q$ lines in this parallel class belong to $B$. Thus $$|C\setminus F_p|\le z(s-1).$$ None of the forced vertices covers any exceptional edge of a group in $I_d$, because those groups use special vertices in direction $d$.

If $z=1$, all positions of this group’s $f(Y)$ must therefore be covered by at most $s-1$ additional vertices. By Lemma <a href="#con-label-action" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="con-label-action">16</a>, their effects can be included in at most $s-1$ subfield lines. This contradicts Lemma <a href="#lem:gadget" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:gadget">12</a>.

<a id="con-two-group-lower"></a>
If $z=2$, the two groups are $1,*$, by <a href="#con-double-directions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="con-double-directions">[con-double-directions]</a>. An additional vertex can now meet both groups, so we use the weighted line-and-point budget instead of counting one line per vertex. For each of these groups let $a_i$ count the additional vertices that cover at least one of its positions and are either special vertices of this group or ordinary vertices in its axis directions $A_i$. Let $b_i'$ count the other additional vertices covering a position of the group. By Lemma <a href="#con-label-action" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="con-label-action">16</a>, the latter vertices each cover only a singleton. The former supply subfield lines. The weighted conclusion of Lemma <a href="#lem:gadget" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:gadget">12</a> gives $$\label{con-two-group-lower}
 a_i+\frac{b_i'}2\ge s
 \qquad (i=1,*).$$

Each additional vertex contributes at most one to the sum of the two left sides. A special vertex is confined to its tagged group. An ordinary axis-direction vertex counted in $a_i$ is a line meeting $P_i$ with direction in $A_i\subseteq D_i$; by <a href="#con-separation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="con-separation">[con-separation]</a> it cannot meet the other group’s $P_j$. It therefore contributes nothing to that other group, even as a singleton. Any vertex of neither exclusive type contributes at most $1/2$ to each group. This accounts for every type of additional vertex, including those covering neither group. Summing <a href="#con-two-group-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="con-two-group-lower">[con-two-group-lower]</a>, we obtain $$2s\le a_1+\frac{b_1'}2+a_*+\frac{b_*'}2
 \le |C\setminus F_p|\le2(s-1),$$ a contradiction.

<a id="pm-h-48"></a>
###### A finite center inside an embedded configuration.

<a id="con-finite-budget"></a>
Now let $p$ be finite, and write $$h_p=|\{\ell\in B:p\in\ell\}|.$$ There are $q+1$ affine lines through $p$, so $$\label{con-finite-budget}
 |F_p|=q+1-h_p,
 \qquad |C\setminus F_p|\le h_p-1.$$ The loss of one in the second inequality comes from the $q+1$ members of a finite pencil and the assumed cover size $q$. If $h_p=0$, the forced vertices already exceed the permitted size of $C$. We may therefore assume $h_p\ge1$ in both finite-center cases.

<a id="con-internal-pencil-size"></a>
Suppose $p\in P_i$, for the necessarily unique such group. Every deletion line through $p$ comes from this group: a line from another group would contradict <a href="#con-separation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="con-separation">[con-separation]</a>, since its direction belongs to that other group’s conflict set. There is at most one such line in each direction of $S_i$, and hence $$\label{con-internal-pencil-size}
 h_p\le s-1.$$ Let $w_p=M_i^{-1}(p-b_i)\in\mathbb F_s^2$, whether or not $w_p\in Y$. We account for the exceptional positions of this group covered by $F_p$: they will all lie on two nonparallel lines in the new coordinates. For a position with base point $y\ne p$, its join with $p$ has a direction in $S_i\cup A_i$. A direction in $S_i$ cannot cover this edge by an ordinary vertex. Any actual forced coverage must therefore lie along one of the two axis-direction lines through $p$. A position based at $p$ itself, if present, lies on both these axes as well.

Since $f$ preserves the two coordinate parallel classes, all these covered positions are contained, in the new coordinates, in the horizontal and vertical lines through $f(w_p)$. The remaining positions are covered by the additional vertices; each can be included in one subfield line by Lemma <a href="#con-label-action" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="con-label-action">16</a>. Equations <a href="#con-finite-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="con-finite-budget">[con-finite-budget]</a> and <a href="#con-internal-pencil-size" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="con-internal-pencil-size">[con-internal-pencil-size]</a> would thus give a cover of $f(Y)$ by at most $$2+(h_p-1)\le s$$ lines containing the two nonparallel coordinate lines. These are legitimate covering resources whether or not their ordinary labels belong to $F_p$: their union contains every position covered by $F_p$. Removing any duplicate replacement lines preserves these two distinct lines and can only decrease the count. This contradicts Lemma <a href="#lem:gadget" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:gadget">12</a>.

<a id="pm-h-49"></a>
###### A finite center outside all embedded configurations.

It remains to consider $p\notin\bigcup_{i\in\mathcal I}P_i$. Here we use the five points on each deletion line, rather than the small-plane cover obstruction. We will find $5h_p$ exceptional edges missed by $F_p$, while each additional vertex covers at most five of them.

Each group contributes at most one deletion line through $p$. Indeed, two distinct such lines cannot be parallel. After undoing $b_i,M_i$, their subfield affine equations have distinct subfield directions. Solving these two equations uses a nonsingular linear system over $\mathbb F_s$, so their intersection lies in $\mathbb F_s^2$. This would place $p$ in $P_i$, a contradiction. Also, deletion lines from different groups cannot coincide, by <a href="#con-separation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="con-separation">[con-separation]</a>.

The $h_p$ deletion lines through $p$ therefore come from $h_p$ distinct groups. From each such group $i$, choose five distinct points of $Y_i$ on its contributing line, possible by Lemma <a href="#con-deletion-count" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="con-deletion-count">13</a>. These give $5h_p$ distinct exceptional edges, by Proposition <a href="#con-intersection" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="con-intersection">15</a>. None is covered by a forced ordinary vertex: its unique join line to $p$ is precisely its contributing deletion line, whose direction is altered for that group.

Any one additional vertex covers at most five of these selected edges. For a special vertex this follows because it belongs to only one group. An ordinary line through $p$ covers none, by the same unique-join and altered-direction argument. Finally, let an ordinary vertex $o_\ell$, with $\ell$ not through $p$, cover some selected edges. The line $\ell$ meets each contributing line in at most one point, so $o_\ell$ covers at most one selected edge from each contributing group. Every such group has a selected point $y\in Y_i\cap\ell$ whose join to $p$ has direction in $S_i$. The third part of Lemma <a href="#con-pool-properties" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="con-pool-properties">14</a>, which applies to all candidates and hence to the selection, bounds the number of these groups by five. This proves the asserted bound for every additional vertex.

By <a href="#con-finite-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="con-finite-budget">[con-finite-budget]</a>, at most $h_p-1$ additional vertices are available. They can cover at most $5(h_p-1)$ of the $5h_p$ selected edges, a final contradiction.

We have ruled out every possible projective center $p$, and hence every vertex cover of size at most $q$. Therefore $\tau(H)\ge q+1$. Conversely, any edge of the nonempty intersecting hypergraph $H$ is itself a vertex cover and has size $q+1$. It follows that $$\tau(H)=q+1.$$ The parameter choices in Section <a href="#sec:placement" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:placement">6</a> give this hypergraph for every sufficiently large odd integer $n$, with $q=s^n$, after fixing any sufficiently large prime $s\equiv2\pmod3$ and a successful $Y$. Choose one such $Y$ for each eligible $s$, and let $n_0(s)\ge3$ exceed all the resulting degree thresholds. This proves Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>. Since there are arbitrarily large odd integers, it also gives infinitely many ranks $r=q+1$. ◻

</details>

  "figures": [
    {
      "caption": "The two label rules for the vertex of $e_{i, w}$ in part $V_{d}$. The left branch uses the embedded point $b_{i} + M_{i}w$; the right branch uses $f(w) = (w_{1}^{3},w_{2})$ in the small plane. For each $d ∈ A_{i}$, two parameters give the same ordinary label exactly when their images under $f$ lie on the same corresponding horizontal or vertical line. The diagram records maps and label choices, rather than geometric positions in the finite plane.",
      "figure_id": "fig:two-coordinates",
      "pdf_page": 33,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:two-coordinates",
          "exact_quote": "The two label rules for the vertex of \\(e_{i,w}\\) in part\n\\(V_d\\).  The left branch uses the embedded point \\(b_i+M_iw\\);\nthe right branch uses \\(f(w)=(w_1^3,w_2)\\) in the small plane.\nFor each \\(d\\in A_i\\), two parameters give the same ord",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[\n  >=Latex,\n  point/.style={draw,rounded corners=2pt,align=center,inner sep=7pt},\n  labelbox/.style={draw,align=center,text width=5.1cm,inner sep=7pt},\n  every node/.style={font=\\small}]\n\\node[point] (w) at (0,0) {$w=(w_1,w_2)\\in Y$};\n\\node[point] (old) at (-3.5,-1.55)\n  {Ambient coordinates\\\\[3pt]$y_{i,w}=b_i+M_iw\\in Y_i$};\n\\node[point] (new) at (3.5,-1.55)\n  {Small-plane coordinates\\\\[3pt]$f(w)=(w_1^3,w_2)\\in f(Y)$};\n\\node[labelbox] (ordinary) at (-3.5,-3.65)\n  {Ordinary vertex in $V_d$\\\\[3pt]$o_{\\ell_d(y_{i,w})}$};\n\\node[labelbox] (special) at (3.5,-3.65)\n  {Special vertex in $V_d$\\\\[3pt]$v_{i,d,L_{t_i(d)}(f(w))}$};\n\\draw[->] (w) -- node[above left,inner sep=2pt,xshift=-4pt,yshift=-2pt] {embed} (old);\n\\draw[->] (w) -- node[above right,inner sep=2pt,xshift=4pt,yshift=-2pt] {apply $f$} (new);\n\\draw[->] (old) -- node[left] {$d\\notin S_i$} (ordinary);\n\\draw[->] (new) -- node[right] {$d\\in S_i$} (special);\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "A flow diagram beginning with a top boxed node containing \"$w = (w_1, w_2) \\in Y$\". An arrow labeled \"embed\" branches down-left to a box containing \"Ambient coordinates\\n$y_{i,w} = b_i + M_i w \\in Y_i$\". From that box, a downward arrow labeled \"$d \\notin S_i$\" leads to a box containing \"Ordinary vertex in $V_d$\\n$o_{\\ell_d(y_{i,w})}$\". A second arrow labeled \"apply $f$\" branches down-right from the top node to a box containing \"Small-plane coordinates\\n$f(w) = (w_1^3, w_2) \\in f(Y)$\". From that box, a downward arrow labeled \"$d \\in S_i$\" leads to a box containing \"Special vertex in $V_d$\\n$v_{i,d,L_{t_i(d)}(f(w))}$\"."
    }
  ],
